=== NOTES 3:0 | Basic Characteristics of the Indian Economy – Features of A Developing Economy
## SUB 0 | Features of a Developing (Underdeveloped) Economy
[0:0:0] # The label
[0:0:1] - India is a **developing economy** (APPSC G2 2003; UPPSC 1995, 2011) – not developed, backward or stagnant; it still shows **underdeveloped** features.
[0:0:2] - India is categorised as a "developing country" mainly because of **low per capita income** – not the service-sector share, quality of manpower or lack of industrial growth.
[0:0:3] # Features of the Indian economy
[0:0:4] - **Low per capita income and unemployment** – the salient features; **massive unemployment**.
[0:0:5] - **Predominance of agriculture:** the Indian economy is **primarily an agricultural economy**; **industrial dominance** marks a developed economy, not India (UP 2004: **I, III and IV**).
[0:0:6] - **Low capital formation** (needs a high savings rate) and **low productivity of labour**.
[0:0:7] - **Inequitable distribution of income, high dependency ratio, slower rate of increase in national income** – underdevelopment features (CGPSC 2017 key: all four listed items, incl. change in banking).
[0:0:8] - **NOT a feature: lack of natural resources** – India has resources; they are under-utilised.
[0:0:9] - **Indicators of a developed country:** high literacy, low death rate, high per capita income – a **high proportion of labour in the primary sector is NOT** one.
[0:0:10] - **Dualistic economy** = **coexistence of a modern and a traditional economy** (not inequality, population growth or resource waste).
[0:0:11] - Agriculture of underdeveloped countries has **surplus labour**; their trade = primary exports, manufactured imports.
[0:0:12] # Causes of underdevelopment
[0:0:13] - **Most important cause:** co-existence of **under-utilised manpower and under-utilised natural resources**.
[0:0:14] - Recorded causes: rapid population growth with high dependency ratio, low human development (HDI rank about **130**), high poverty – **NOT mainly the working efficiency of the population** (APPSC G2 Mains 2017).
[0:0:15] - **Vicious circle of poverty (Ragnar Nurkse):** low income → low saving → low investment → low productivity → low income; the vicious circle argument points out the **supply-side connection between productivity and income**.
[0:0:16] - **Labour-surplus economy:** surplus farm labour whose marginal productivity is near zero.
[0:0:17] - **Disguised unemployment:** more workers than needed; marginal productivity of the extra workers **zero**; removing them would **not** reduce output; mainly in **agriculture** and the unorganised sector.
[0:0:18] - Capital formation in a developing economy can rise only with an adequate **savings rate**.
[0:0:19] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.29-30; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 02 p.43 (vicious circle); Rocket Indian Economy #36 p.53, #33 p.49; app notes 3:0 subs 1-3
## SUB 1 | Economic Systems – India's Mixed Economy
[0:1:0] # Types of economy
[0:1:1] TABLE head: Type | Who owns / who decides | Example
  [0:1:1.0] Capitalist (market, free) | Production and distribution in the hands of private individuals; prices by market forces; economic inequality is a feature | USA
  [0:1:1.1] Socialist | All productive resources owned and controlled by the government; collective ownership, equitable distribution | Former USSR
  [0:1:1.2] Command (controlled, planned) | A central plan replaces the market; the government decides what, how and for whom | Former USSR, Cuba, North Korea
  [0:1:1.3] Traditional | Custom and tradition decide what, how and for whom | –
  [0:1:1.4] Gandhian | Decentralised, self-contained villages, cottage industries | Gandhian Plan 1944 – S.N. Agarwal
  [0:1:1.5] Mixed | Coexistence of public and private sectors | India, UK
  [0:1:1.6] Dual | Modern sector beside a traditional sector | Lewis model
[0:1:2] # India = mixed economy
[0:1:3] - **After Independence India followed the path of a mixed economy** – public and private sectors coexist under State regulation; it is neither purely capitalist, socialist, Gandhian nor free (asked about 12 times: APPSC ASO 2017, AEs 2009, G2, Constable 2019, CDS 2019, UPPSC).
[0:1:4] - **Meaning:** co-existence of **public and private sectors** – NOT co-existence of small and large industries, labour- and capital-intensive industries, national and foreign companies, agriculture and industry, or military and civilian rule.
[0:1:5] - **How the State regulates** the private sector: **taxes, subsidies and credit**; a high level of public investment in infrastructure is a precondition for private-sector growth.
[0:1:6] - **Socialistic pattern of society** (Avadi 1955, IPR 1956) was to come about **through the mixed economy** (APPSC JL 2004).
[0:1:7] - The Indian model of development protects the interest of **both State and Person** (UP 2013).
[0:1:8] - A **pragmatic middle path** between liberal-capitalist, Soviet and Gandhian models; growth by democratic means with a social order based on justice.
[0:1:9] - **"Commanding heights"** under the State: **heavy industry, infrastructure and finance**; private sector in consumer goods under regulation.
[0:1:10] - **Joint sector** = **one enterprise jointly owned** by government and private investors (a mixed economy is the whole system).
[0:1:11] - **Public sector** = government ownership or control; **private sector** = private ownership or control.
[0:1:12] # Planned, market, open and closed
[0:1:13] - **Centrally planned economy:** the government makes all important decisions on production, exchange and consumption (UPPSC 2002 ✓); in a **market system** prices are set by demand and supply, **not agreed by the government** ✗ → **Only I**.
[0:1:14] - **Closed economy:** **neither exports nor imports** take place (UPSC 2011); open economy trades with others.
[0:1:15] > UPPSC 2024: "Indian economy is underdeveloped" ✓ (a developing economy with underdeveloped features) and "mixed" ✓; "Keynes is the Father of Economics" ✗ → **only 1 and 2**.
[0:1:16] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 02 p.7-8 (types of economy); NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.29-30; Rocket Indian Economy #57 p.85; app notes 3:0 sub 0
## SUB 2 | Basic Concepts of Economics and Economists
[0:2:0] TABLE head: Name | Remember for
  [0:2:0.0] Adam Smith (Scottish) | Father of (Modern) Economics / of capitalism – *Wealth of Nations* (1776); not Keynes
  [0:2:0.1] J.M. Keynes | *General Theory*; modern macroeconomics; paradox of thrift – if all save more, income falls and total saving may not rise
  [0:2:0.2] Joan Robinson | *The Accumulation of Capital* (1956)
  [0:2:0.3] W. Arthur Lewis | Dual-sector model: surplus labour from the subsistence to the modern sector
  [0:2:0.4] A.L. Bowley | Averages = "statistical constants which enable us to comprehend in a single effort the significance of the whole"
[0:2:1] - **"Economics"** comes from the **Greek** *oikonomia* (oikos = household, nomos = management).
[0:2:2] # Micro concepts asked
[0:2:3] - **Fixed cost** does not vary with output – **expenditure on machines**; raw material, labour wages and traffic costs vary.
[0:2:4] - **Perfect substitutes** = completely interchangeable goods; perfect complements are used together.
[0:2:5] - **Giffen good:** inferior good; income and substitution effects oppose – demand can be inversely or positively related to price; if the **income effect is stronger**, demand moves **with** price → **both** statements true.
[0:2:6] - **Total and marginal product:** TP rising at an increasing rate → MP rises; at a diminishing rate → MP falls; TP maximum → **MP zero**; TP falling → **MP negative** (not positive).
[0:2:7] - **Law of increasing returns = law of decreasing cost.**
[0:2:8] - **Marginal revenue:** MR > 0 → TR rises; **MR = 0 → TR maximum**; MR < 0 → TR falls (all three true).
[0:2:9] - **Price elasticity of demand** depends on the **nature of the good** and the **availability of close substitutes**.
[0:2:10] - **Economic models** simplify reality, represent a whole or part of a theory and show cause and effect; they can be expressed in words, diagrams, tables or equations – **not only equations**.
[0:2:11] - **Unit root** is a concept of **time-series models** (Dickey-Fuller test): a series with a unit root is non-stationary – not of cross-section or structural-form models.
[0:2:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.30 (Adam Smith); Rocket Indian Economy #36 p.53 (economists); en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unit_root (Dickey & Fuller 1979); NCERT Class 12 Introductory Microeconomics ch.2-4; app notes 3:0 sub 3
## SUB 3 | Micro Basics – Demand, Utility, Elasticity and Consumer Choice
[0:3:0] # Definitions and laws
[0:3:1] - **Lionel Robbins** (1932): economics studies the relationship between **unlimited wants (ends) and scarce means** with alternative uses (scarcity definition).
[0:3:2] - **Behavioural economics** applies **psychological insights** into human behaviour to explain economic decisions (Kahneman, Thaler).
[0:3:3] - **Saturation point:** total utility is at its maximum and **marginal utility is zero** (beyond it MU turns negative).
[0:3:4] - **Consumer equilibrium (two goods):** MUx/Px = MUy/Py – if the MU of the last unit of X is twice that of Y, equilibrium needs the **price of X to be twice the price of Y**.
[0:3:5] - **Budget line:** a point **below (inside)** the budget line costs less than income – the consumer is **not spending all income**; a point on the line spends it all.
[0:3:6] # Demand
[0:3:7] - **Normal goods:** demand rises with income (positive income effect); **inferior goods:** demand falls as income rises.
[0:3:8] - The demand curve **slopes downward** and is usually drawn **convex to the origin**.
[0:3:9] - **Demand for a good rises** when the **price of a substitute rises**; it falls when the price of a complement rises or (for an inferior good) when income rises; a fall in its own price raises the quantity demanded (UPSC key: **1 and 4 only**).
[0:3:10] - **Supply curve of a farmer:** everything is held constant **except the price of the commodity itself** (that is the variable on the curve).
[0:3:11] # Elasticity
[0:3:12] TABLE head: Case | Meaning
  [0:3:12.0] Zero price elasticity | Quantity demanded does not change at all whatever the price change (vertical demand curve)
  [0:3:12.1] Zero cross elasticity | Purchases of one good do not change when the other's price changes – unrelated (independent) goods
  [0:3:12.2] Positive / negative cross elasticity | Substitutes / complements
  [0:3:12.3] Tangent demand curves | A straight-line and a curved demand curve tangent at a point have the same elasticity there
[0:3:13] - **Consumer surplus** (Marshall) – what consumers would pay minus what they pay; used in **tax policy** (burden of commodity taxes), pricing of public utilities.
[0:3:14] ~~ Sources: OnlyIAS NCERT Economy 03 p.14-21 (NCERT Class XII Introductory Microeconomics ch.2: budget line, normal and inferior goods, elasticity); NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 02 p.47; L. Robbins, An Essay on the Nature and Significance of Economic Science (1932); D. Salvatore, Schaum's Outline of Microeconomic Theory ch.2-4
## SUB 4 | Micro Basics – Production, Costs and Factor Pricing
[0:4:0] # Production
[0:4:1] - **Law of diminishing returns:** adding more units of a variable factor to a fixed factor → **marginal product eventually diminishes**.
[0:4:2] - **Constant returns to scale** need **all inputs** raised in the same proportion; raising **labour alone by 10%** with capital fixed raises output by **less than 10%** (diminishing returns).
[0:4:3] - **CES production function:** the **elasticity of substitution** between inputs is **constant** (Cobb-Douglas is the special case with elasticity 1).
[0:4:4] # Costs
[0:4:5] TABLE head: Cost | Meaning / example
  [0:4:5.0] Explicit cost | Money actually paid to buy or hire factors of production
  [0:4:5.1] Implicit / opportunity cost | Return forgone on own resources – e.g. the interest he could earn by lending out his own funds
  [0:4:5.2] Fixed cost (farm) | Does not vary with output – land rent, depreciation, interest on fixed capital (seeds, fertiliser, hired labour are variable)
  [0:4:5.3] AFC | Falls continuously (rectangular hyperbola) – the only cost curve that is not U-shaped (AVC, ATC, MC are U-shaped)
[0:4:6] - **LAC falls as output expands** because of **economies of scale**.
[0:4:7] - **Increasing cost industry:** factor prices rise as the industry uses more factors (prices and quantities move in the **same direction**).
[0:4:8] - **Cash** = notes, coins, bank balances and cash equivalents; long-term **investments** are not cash.
[0:4:9] # Factor pricing
[0:4:10] - **Value of marginal product (VMP) of labour** = **marginal product of labour × price** of output.
[0:4:11] - **Quasi-rent** (Marshall) = total revenue − total variable cost = fixed cost + profit → **greater than the firm's total profit**.
[0:4:12] ~~ Sources: OnlyIAS NCERT Economy 03 p.28-35 (NCERT Class XII Microeconomics ch.3: production function, returns, cost curves); D. Salvatore, Schaum's Outline of Microeconomic Theory ch.6-7, 13; A. Koutsoyiannis, Modern Microeconomics ch.3-4 (CES, quasi-rent)
## SUB 5 | Micro Basics – Market Structures, Oligopoly and Game Theory
[0:5:0] # Competition and monopoly
[0:5:1] - **Perfect market:** **large number of buyers and sellers**, homogeneous product, free entry and exit, perfect knowledge.
[0:5:2] - **Monopolistic competition and monopoly** share a **downward-sloping demand curve**; in long-run equilibrium the monopolistic competitor **never** produces at the lowest point of LAC (tangency on the falling part → excess capacity).
[0:5:3] - A pure monopolist keeps **pure profits in the long run** because of **blocked entry**.
[0:5:4] - When a competitive industry becomes a monopoly, part of **consumer surplus** goes to the monopolist as **economic profit** (the rest is deadweight loss).
[0:5:5] - **Contestable markets** (Baumol): even a few firms behave competitively **if entry is absolutely free and exit entirely costless**.
[0:5:6] # Duopoly models
[0:5:7] TABLE head: Model | Each firm assumes the rival keeps … | Note
  [0:5:7.0] Cournot | Its quantity (output) constant | Interdependence not recognised; stable solution
  [0:5:7.1] Bertrand | Its price constant | Price war down to competitive price
  [0:5:7.2] Edgeworth | Its price constant | Price oscillates – no stable solution
[0:5:8] # Game theory
[0:5:9] - **Zero-sum game:** one player's gain = the other's loss.
[0:5:10] - **Maximin:** choose the strategy that **maximises the minimum gain** (minimax = minimise the maximum loss).
[0:5:11] ~~ Sources: OnlyIAS NCERT Economy 03 p.47-60 (perfect competition, non-competitive markets); NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 02 p.47, p.55 (monopolistic competition); D. Salvatore, Schaum's Outline of Microeconomic Theory ch.9-11; A. Koutsoyiannis, Modern Microeconomics ch.9 (Cournot, Bertrand, Edgeworth)
## SUB 6 | Micro Basics – Welfare Economics, General Equilibrium and Linear Programming
[0:6:0] # Pareto and general equilibrium
[0:6:1] - **Pareto's welfare analysis** relates to the production function and **utility** (no one can be made better off without making someone worse off).
[0:6:2] - **General equilibrium of production and exchange** (2 persons, 2 goods): **MRT(xy) = MRS(xy) of A = MRS(xy) of B**.
[0:6:3] - **Consumption (exchange) contract curve** = locus of general-equilibrium points of exchange.
[0:6:4] - **Grand utility-possibility curve** = locus of Pareto optimality in **both production and consumption**.
[0:6:5] - **Social welfare function** needs an **ethical (value) judgement** – Pareto optimality alone cannot rank its points.
[0:6:6] - **Productive efficiency** = **factor-price (allocative) efficiency + technical efficiency**.
[0:6:7] # Shadow prices and LP
[0:6:8] - **Shadow (accounting) prices** = imputed values of costs and benefits, used in public-sector project appraisal as a **sound basis for allocation decisions**.
[0:6:9] - In **linear programming** the shadow price = **change in the objective function per unit change in a constraint**; LP assumes linearity, i.e. **constant returns** – increasing returns to scale is **not** an assumption.
[0:6:10] ~~ Sources: D. Salvatore, Schaum's Outline of Microeconomic Theory ch.14-15 (general equilibrium, welfare); A. Koutsoyiannis, Modern Microeconomics ch.22-23; Little-Mirrlees / UNIDO project appraisal (shadow prices)
## SUB 7 | India at Independence and the Colonial Legacy
[0:7:0] - **Colonial rule** (from **Plassey, 1757**) turned India from a textile exporter into a **raw-material supplier and captive market** – India was **NOT a supplier of manufactured goods** under British rule; the economy **stagnated** (UPPSC 2013).
[0:7:1] - **Paul Bairoch:** India made about **24.5% of the world's manufactures in 1750** (APPSC G2 2017) → about **1.7% by 1900** (deindustrialisation).
[0:7:2] - **Angus Maddison:** India's share of world GDP **22.6% (1700) → 3.8% (1952)**.
[0:7:3] - **First National Income Committee (1951):** 1948-49 national income **₹8,710 crore**; per capita **₹225**.
[0:7:4] - **Agriculture:** about **72% of the workforce (1951)**; about half of GDP.
[0:7:5] - **Weak industrial base:** capital goods only **one-fourth** of industrial output.
[0:7:6] - **Literacy below 16%**; **life expectancy 32 years** (1947).
[0:7:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.30; P. Bairoch, "International Industrialization Levels from 1750 to 1980" (J. European Econ. Hist. 1982) via en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economic_history_of_India; app notes 3:0 sub 3, 3:1 sub 6
## SUB 8 | Ownership, Institutional Sectors and Savings
[0:8:0] TABLE head: Sector | Ownership
  [0:8:0.0] Public | Government (general government + public corporations)
  [0:8:0.1] Private | Private corporations + households
  [0:8:0.2] Joint | Government + private investors in one firm
  [0:8:0.3] Cooperative | Owned by its members
[0:8:1] - **Largest share of national income** in India's mixed economy: **private sector**.
[0:8:2] - GVA is compiled for **four institutional sectors**: general government, public corporations, private corporations, households – **cooperatives are not a separate one**.
[0:8:3] - **Gross domestic savings:** the **household sector** contributes the largest share (APPSC G1 2007) – household > private corporate > public.
[0:8:4] - **SI 2017 match:** gross domestic savings (2011) **31.1%**; gross capital formation (2011) **36.6%**; unemployment rate (2004-05) **8.36%**; population growth (2001-11) **1.64%** a year → **A-IV, B-I, C-II, D-III**.
[0:8:5] - **Private corporate investment:** **12.3% of GDP (FY23) → 11.2% (FY24)**; its share of GFCF at a decadal low of **33%** (Aug 2026).
[0:8:6] ~~ Sources: NSO National Accounts Statistics (institutional sectors, savings); Census 2011 (1.64% exponential growth); app notes 3:0 sub 5
## SUB 9 | India's World Standing and the AP Profile
[0:9:0] TABLE head: Measure | Position
  [0:9:0.0] Nominal GDP | 6th (IMF WEO, April 2026)
  [0:9:0.1] PPP GDP | 3rd, after the USA and China
  [0:9:0.2] World Bank income group | Lower-middle income (July 2026)
[0:9:1] - **World Bank classification:** by **GNI per capita** (not GDP) using the **Atlas method** (exchange rate averaged over **three years**); revised every **1 July**.
[0:9:2] - **World Development Report (1996):** low-income economies = per capita GNP in 1994 of **US $725 or less** (IAS 1998).
[0:9:3] - **GDP growth** (2022-23 base): **7.2% (2023-24), 7.1% (2024-25), 7.7% (2025-26)**.
[0:9:4] TABLE head: Indicator (2025-26) | Andhra Pradesh
  [0:9:4.0] Per capita income | ₹2,94,507
  [0:9:4.1] GVA shares | Services 43%, agriculture & allied about 33%, industry 24%
  [0:9:4.2] Top district in PCI | Visakhapatnam
[0:9:5] - **State per capita income = NSDP ÷ population.**
[0:9:6] ~~ Sources: World Bank, World Development Report 1996 (classification of economies); World Bank country classification (July 2026); AP Socio-Economic Survey 2025-26 via app notes 3:0 subs 9-10
## SUB 10 | Dual Economy – Organised vs Unorganised; Regional and Demographic Features
[0:10:0] - **Organised sector:** formal, regulated work with **social security**; **unorganised:** informal, largely **cash-based**.
[0:10:1] - Unorganised sector: **over 80% of workers**, about **45% of output**.
[0:10:2] - **Census 2011:** population **121.09 crore**; **68.8% rural, 31.2% urban**; sex ratio **940** (933 in 2001).
[0:10:3] - **Work participation rate 39.8%** (Census 2011) – higher in less developed States such as AP and Chhattisgarh because **subsistence activities need more manual work**.
[0:10:4] - **Regional imbalance:** inter-state Gini of per capita income **0.230 (1994-95) → 0.261**; Telangana's PCI = **1.35 times** AP's (2024-25).
[0:10:5] - **Demography:** TFR **1.9**, below the **replacement level 2.1**; median age about **36 by 2036**; dependency ratio expected to fall from **65% to 54%** over a decade; **demographic dividend peaks around 2030**.
[0:10:6] ~~ Sources: Census 2011 (Primary Census Abstract); NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 02 p.19; app notes 3:0 subs 7-8

=== OWN MCQs of 3:0 (64)
[n98a55b47241] sub0
  Capital formation in a developing economy can be increased only when there is an adequate level of which of the following?
  Agricultural population ratio | Rate of disguised unemployment | Dependency ratio | *Savings rate
  x: A high savings rate is necessary to generate the resources required for capital formation and investment.
[nb546e7f89d6] sub0
  A common criticism of India's development is that its large GDP coexists with an HDI rank of about how much?
  80 | 100 | *130 | 150
  x: The criticism cites a large GDP alongside an HDI rank of 130, showing that economic size does not ensure equivalent human development.
[fd5ff9f6c4c18] sub0
  What is an economy with surplus farm labour whose marginal productivity is near zero called?
  *A labour-surplus economy | A mixed economy | A capitalist economy | A command economy
  x: A labour-surplus economy has surplus farm labour with near-zero marginal productivity, the Lewis setting.
[n1ee21acc0ad] sub0
  What is the situation called in which more workers are engaged in agriculture than needed, so that the marginal productivity of some workers is zero?
  Structural inflation | *Disguised unemployment | Frictional unemployment | Seasonal employment
  x: Disguised unemployment exists when surplus workers are employed and the marginal productivity of labour is zero. It is mainly associated with agriculture and the unorganised sector.
[f3c9739e4499c] sub0
  Consider the following statements about disguised unemployment:
1. The marginal productivity of the extra workers is zero.
2. Removing the extra workers would reduce output.
3. It is found mainly in agriculture.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  1 and 2 only | 2 and 3 only | *1 and 3 only | 1, 2 and 3
  x: Under disguised unemployment extra workers add nothing, so removing them would not cut output; it is mainly found in agriculture.
[n76030b537cf] sub0
  The vicious circle of poverty, associated with Ragnar Nurkse, is correctly represented by which one of the following sequences?
  Low income → high saving → low investment → high productivity → low income | *Low income → low saving → low investment → low productivity → low income | High income → low saving → high investment → low productivity → high income | Low income → high investment → low saving → high productivity → high income
  x: Ragnar Nurkse's vicious circle is low income leading to low saving, low investment and low productivity, which in turn leads back to low income.
[ne421008690f] sub0
  Which one of the following is regarded as the most important cause of underdevelopment of the Indian economy?
  High density of population | Lack of capital alone | *Co-existence of under-utilised manpower and under-utilised natural resources | Low per capita income
  x: The key answer is the co-existence of under-utilised manpower and under-utilised natural resources. High density, lack of capital and low per capita income are each only part of the picture and are the trap options when taken alone.
[gcf1eaadbd4d4] sub0
  Which of the following is NOT a main cause of underdevelopment of the Indian economy?
  Rapid population growth with high dependency | Low human development | High poverty | *Working efficiency of the population
  x: Population growth, low human development and poverty are recorded causes; working efficiency of the population is not a main cause.
[n18955ba27ea] sub1
  Which of the following most accurately distinguishes a mixed economy from a joint sector?
  A mixed economy is jointly owned by government and private investors, whereas a joint sector merely regulates markets. | A mixed economy has only public ownership, whereas a joint sector has only private ownership. | *A mixed economy refers to public and private sectors coexisting in the economy, whereas a joint sector refers to one enterprise jointly owned by government and private investors. | A mixed economy is a command economy, whereas a joint sector is a capitalist economy.
  x: A mixed economy denotes the coexistence of public and private sectors. A joint sector is a specific enterprise jointly owned by government and private investors.
[n5fc7eb7ea20] sub1
  Which of the following is NOT correct about types of economies?
  The Gandhian model envisages decentralised, self-contained villages with cottage industries | A socialist economy emphasises collective ownership and equitable distribution | *In a command economy, custom and tradition decide what, how and for whom to produce | Capitalism is marked by private ownership, market forces and economic inequality
  x: In a command economy the government's central plan decides production; custom and tradition decide it in a traditional economy.
[n891a6766ab9] sub1
  With whom is the Gandhian Plan of 1944 associated?
  M. Visvesvaraya | *S. N. Agarwal | M. N. Roy | J. R. D. Tata
  x: The Gandhian Plan of 1944 is associated with S. N. Agarwal.
[n4103c1295fb] sub1
  India's economic system is described as a pragmatic middle path between which of the following models?
  *Liberal-capitalist, Soviet and Gandhian models | Colonial, imperial and protectionist models | Traditional, feudal and mercantilist models | Agrarian, industrial and financial models
  x: India follows a mixed-economy approach that is described as a pragmatic middle path between liberal-capitalist, Soviet and Gandhian models. It seeks growth by democratic means along with a social order based on justice.
[nb53bf655042] sub1
  In a mixed economy, how does the State mainly regulate the private sector?
  Nationalisation of all industries | Rationing of all consumer goods | *Taxes, subsidies and credit | Compulsory state procurement of output
  x: The State regulates the private sector in a mixed economy through taxation, subsidies and credit.
[nindian-economy-57-12] sub1
  Government ownership or control is the basic idea of which sector?
  Private Sector | *Public Sector | Real Sector | Financial Sector
  x: Government ownership or control defines the public sector; private ownership or control defines the private sector.
[ndd6553a8614] sub1
  In India's middle path, what did the 'commanding heights' under State control refer to?
  *Heavy industry, infrastructure and finance | Agriculture, handicrafts and village industries | Consumer goods, retail trade and tourism | Small-scale industries and handlooms
  x: India's middle path kept State control over the commanding heights, namely heavy industry, infrastructure and finance, while allowing a regulated private sector in consumer goods.
[faf66d39fd536] sub1
  In a command (controlled) economy, what replaces the market?
  Custom and tradition | *A central plan | The private profit motive | Self-contained villages
  x: In a command economy a central plan replaces the market and the government decides what, how and for whom to produce.
[f10553b063ceb] sub1
  In a traditional economy, what decides what, how and for whom to produce?
  A central planning authority | Market prices | *Custom and tradition | Public sector enterprises
  x: A traditional economy relies on custom and tradition to answer the basic economic questions.
[g3bb157c1c6a0] sub1

=== NOTES 3:1 | Structural Change – Sectoral Composition of Output and Employment
## SUB 0 | Sectoral Shares of GDP – Trend and Ranking
[1:0:0] # Order and trend
[1:0:1] - **Decreasing order of contribution to GDP now: services > industry > agriculture** (UPSC 2007, NDA 2014, UPPCS 2017) – the **tertiary (service) sector is the largest source of national income** in the 21st century.
[1:0:2] - **At Independence agriculture** was the largest contributor (about half of GDP); over the plan period the composition **shifted in favour of the service sector**.
[1:0:3] - **As the economy develops the tertiary share rises**; since 1980 it has shown an **increasing trend**; agriculture's contribution is **decreasing**.
[1:0:4] TABLE head: Year | Agriculture / primary | Industry | Services
  [1:0:4.0] 1950-51 | over half (SI 2017 key: 56.5%) | about 13% | 30.3%
  [1:0:4.1] 1980-81 → 1990-91 | 38.1% → 34.0% | 25.9% → 24.9% | 37.65% → 42.55%
  [1:0:4.2] 1991 | about 29% | – | –
  [1:0:4.3] 2001-03 | about 26% (APPSC JL 2003 key) | – | 50.37% (2000-01)
  [1:0:4.4] 2006-07 (Economic Survey 2006-07) | 18.5% | 26.4% (between 25 and 30) | 55.1%
  [1:0:4.5] 2012-13 | about 13.7% (SI 2017 key 13.6%) | – | –
  [1:0:4.6] 2017-18 | about 17% | 29.2% | about 54-55% (55.2% AE; APPSC key 54.4%)
  [1:0:4.7] 2025-26 | 17.88% (about 18%) | manufacturing stagnant | 54.3%
[1:0:5] - **Agriculture around 2008-13: about 15%** of GDP (BPSC 2008, UPRO 2013 key) – now about 18% after the revisions.
[1:0:6] # New GDP series (base 2022-23, Feb 2026)
[1:0:7] - **2024-25, current prices: primary 21.4%, secondary 25.8%, tertiary 52.9%** – figures to quote now; the new series shows higher agriculture and industry shares, lower services.
[1:0:8] - **The 21.4% primary share includes mining**, so it is not comparable with the old agriculture-forestry-fishing share.
[1:0:9] - **Manufacturing 14.7%** in the new series (not 14.3%); built from **ASI** (factories) + **ASUSE** (unincorporated units).
[1:0:10] # Sub-sectors
[1:0:11] - **2000-01 (constant prices) order:** agriculture-forestry-fishing > trade-hotels-transport-communication > manufacturing > finance-insurance-real estate-business services (UPSC 2002: **1, 3, 2, 4**).
[1:0:12] - **Highest services share in 2006:** trade, hotels, transport and communication; **fastest-growing service 2006-2010: communication**.
[1:0:13] - **Mining and quarrying ≈ 3% of GVA** in 2016-17 at 2011-12 prices (about 2.4% at current prices) – small output share, key raw-material supplier.
[1:0:14] - **1999-2008 (1999-2000 prices):** within the primary sector the share of **forestry** declined steeply (APPSC G2 2011 key).
[1:0:15] - **Fishing and aquaculture** (old series): **0.8% (2011-12) → 1.3% (2022-23)**; manufacturing 17.4% → 14.3%.
[1:0:16] - **Agriculture's importance** shows in its contribution to **national income and employment, industrial raw materials, trade and foodgrains – all**; its large contribution to GNP is in terms of **value of output**, not exports or revenue.
[1:0:17] - **Highest growth in industrial production** (APPSC G1 2003): **manufacturing**.
[1:0:18] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 14 p.4; NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 22 p.12; Economic Survey 2006-07 ch.1 (indiabudget.gov.in/budget_archive/es2006-07); Economic Survey 2017-18 Vol.2 ch.9 (services 55.2%); NSO Second Advance Estimates 2025-26 (27 Feb 2026); app notes 3:1 subs 3-4
## SUB 1 | Sector Classification – Primary, Secondary, Tertiary and Beyond
[1:1:0] TABLE head: Sector | Other name | Activities
  [1:1:0.0] Primary | Agricultural sector; most dependent on natural resources | Agriculture, livestock, dairy farm, forestry, fishing, mining and quarrying, mineral exploration
  [1:1:0.1] Secondary | Industrial sector | Manufacturing (large, medium, small), textile mills, weaving cloth, processing, construction, electricity, gas, water supply
  [1:1:0.2] Tertiary | Service sector | Trade, marketing, transport, storage (incl. storage of farm produce), communication, hotels, banking, finance, real estate, public administration and defence, education, health, social work, personal services
  [1:1:0.3] Quaternary | Knowledge sector | R&D, information, software, consulting
  [1:1:0.4] Quinary | Top decision-making | Government policy, scientific research
[1:1:1] - **Water supply = secondary** (industrial) sector (APPSC G1), not tertiary.
[1:1:2] - **Real estate** is a tertiary activity – **not part of manufacturing**.
[1:1:3] - **Service sector** includes **transport and communication and hotels** – not mining and quarrying, forestry or fishing (UPPCS 2004: **II and III**).
[1:1:4] - **UPSC 2024 pairs:** storage = secondary ✗ (tertiary); dairy farm = primary ✓; mineral exploration = tertiary ✗ (primary); weaving cloth = secondary ✓ → **only two**.
[1:1:5] # Real sector vs financial sector
[1:1:6] TABLE head: Basis | Real sector | Financial sector
  [1:1:6.0] Involves | Production and flow of goods and services | Money and financial assets
  [1:1:6.1] Examples | Farmers harvesting crops, textile mills making fabric | Bank lending, rupee-denominated bonds overseas
[1:1:7] - UPSC 2022: real sector = **1 and 2 only**.
[1:1:8] # GVA groups (national accounts)
[1:1:9] TABLE head: Group | Components
  [1:1:9.0] Agriculture and allied | Agriculture, livestock, forestry, fishing
  [1:1:9.1] Industry | Mining and quarrying, manufacturing, electricity-gas-water supply, construction
  [1:1:9.2] Services | Trade-hotels-transport-communication; finance-real estate-professional services; public administration, defence, other services
[1:1:10] - **Mining = primary** in the three-sector scheme but counted under **Industry** in GVA groups and the AP Socio-Economic Survey.
[1:1:11] - **Compilers:** GDP and sectoral GVA – **CSO, now NSO (MoSPI)**; State GSDP and sector GVA – **Directorate of Economics and Statistics** of each State; ASI and ASUSE – **NSO** (not the State DES).
[1:1:12] ~~ Sources: Rocket Indian Economy #57 p.84-85; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 02 p.59-61; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 03 p.11; NCERT Class 10 Economics ch.2; app notes 3:1 subs 0-1
## SUB 2 | Output vs Employment – the Mismatch
[1:2:0] - **The maximum number of people work in the primary sector** (agriculture) – the **largest employer**, though it gives the least output; the tertiary sector gives the most output (APPSC JL 2004, AD 2017).
[1:2:1] - Agriculture employs **46.1%** of workers (PLFS 2023-24); earlier **more than 50%** of the working population depended on the primary sector (WBCS 2017).
[1:2:2] TABLE head: Sector (2021-22) | Share of GDP | Share of workforce
  [1:2:2.0] Primary | 21.82% | 43.96%
  [1:2:2.1] Tertiary | 53.89% | 33.80%
[1:2:3] - Services were **22.82%** of employment in 1993-94 (agriculture 64.75%).
[1:2:4] - **Manufacturing** employs only **11.4%** of workers (2023-24); **MSMEs** are the second-largest employer after agriculture.
[1:2:5] - **Organised-sector employment growth declined** in recent years mainly because the **manufacturing share of GDP did not increase** (APPSC AD 2017).
[1:2:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 22 p.12; PLFS Annual Report 2023-24 (MoSPI); app notes 3:1 sub 5
## SUB 3 | Structural Change and Services-led Growth
[1:3:0] - **Structural change** = changing shares of the primary, secondary and tertiary sectors in national income; it occurred **because the process of economic growth was initiated** (APPSC G1: A and R true, **R explains A**).
[1:3:1] - **Shifting of workers from agriculture to other sectors** = an indication of **economic growth** (APPSC G2 2011), not backwardness.
[1:3:2] TABLE head: Model | Author | Prediction
  [1:3:2.0] Three-sector | Colin Clark and Allan Fisher | As income rises, labour and output shift primary → secondary → tertiary
  [1:3:2.1] Dual-sector | W. Arthur Lewis (1954) | Surplus farm labour moves to modern industry until it runs out – the Lewis turning point
[1:3:3] - **Post-1991 growth was service-led** – not import-, export- or agriculture-led (APPSC 2018); in **1992-2001 services** grew fastest and contributed most to GDP.
[1:3:4] TABLE head: Term | Meaning
  [1:3:4.0] Missing middle | Agriculture → services without a manufacturing-led stage
  [1:3:4.1] Premature deindustrialisation | Services generally require high skills and may not absorb semi-skilled farm workers → jobless growth
  [1:3:4.2] Premature leapfrogging | Services absorb value while manufacturing fails to absorb surplus farm labour
  [1:3:4.3] Nominal vs real share | Fall in manufacturing's nominal share = relative-price effect (Economic Survey 2025-26)
  [1:3:4.4] Share logic | A sector growing slower than GDP loses share even if its output rises
[1:3:5] - **EAC-PM Chairman** (Nov 2025): services-led growth cannot be sustained without a manufacturing base.
[1:3:6] - India: **2.9%** of global manufacturing value added; **1.8%** of world merchandise exports; **44th** on the Economic Complexity Index.
[1:3:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 22 p.12; Economic Survey 2025-26 (economic-survey-2025-26-full); app notes 3:1 subs 2, 6
## SUB 4 | Manufacturing Targets and Recent Sectoral Growth
[1:4:0] TABLE head: Policy | Year | Target
  [1:4:0.0] National Manufacturing Policy | 2011 | Manufacturing 25% of GDP by 2022; 100 million jobs
  [1:4:0.1] Make in India | 25 Sep 2014 | 25% of GDP – deadline extended from 2022 to 2025
[1:4:1] - Target not met: the share fell to 14.3% (2022-23, old series).
[1:4:2] TABLE head: Agriculture growth | 9th Plan | 10th Plan | 11th Plan
  [1:4:2.0] Growth | 2.5% | 2.4% | 3.6% (highest)
[1:4:3] - **11th Plan target:** **4%** agricultural growth (achieved about 3.6-3.7%).
[1:4:4] TABLE head: Sector (real) | 2025-26 | Q1 2026-27
  [1:4:4.0] Manufacturing | 12.5% | 9.2%
  [1:4:4.1] Services | 8.9% | 10.0%
[1:4:5] - Q1 2026-27 leaders: **finance, real estate, IT and professional services (12.1%)**.
[1:4:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 14 p.4 (11th Plan 3.6%); NSO quarterly estimates (Aug 2026) via app notes 3:1 subs 7-8
## SUB 5 | Andhra Pradesh – GSVA Composition
[1:5:0] TABLE head: Year (source) | Agriculture & allied | Industry | Services
  [1:5:0.0] 2024-25 (AP Budget 2026-27) | 37% | 21% | 42%
  [1:5:0.1] 2025-26 (AP SES 2025-26) | about 33% | 24% | 43%
[1:5:1] - AP has **a farm share well above the national share** (India below 20%).
[1:5:2] - **AP growth 2025-26 (constant prices):** fishing and aquaculture fastest within agriculture (17.69%); **construction** fastest within industry (14.18%); **public administration** lowest (0.05%).
[1:5:3] - **Manufacturing only about 11% of GSDP** → **Industrial Development Policy 4.0 (2024-29)**.
[1:5:4] - Telangana's services grew from 1.25 to 1.47 times AP's (2013-14 to 2024-25), led by **real estate, IT and trade-hotels**.
[1:5:5] ~~ Sources: AP Budget 2026-27 speech; AP Socio-Economic Survey 2025-26 via app notes 3:1 sub 9

=== OWN MCQs of 3:1 (78)
[na1340938333] sub0

  *Tertiary sector | Secondary sector | Primary sector | Mining and quarrying
  x: In 2024-25 the tertiary sector accounted for 52.9 per cent, against 25.8 per cent for the secondary and 21.4 per cent for the primary sector.
[gfd1ac45499b9] sub0
  About what share of India's GVA does agriculture contribute (2025-26)?
  About 12% | *About 18% | About 26% | About 35%
  x: Agriculture contributes about 18% of GVA (17.88%, 2025-26) but employs about 46% of workers.
[g053e8e47296b] sub0
  Consider the following statements about mining and quarrying in India:
1. It is a primary activity in the three-sector scheme.
2. It contributes more than 10% of India's GVA.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  *1 only | 2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: Mining is primary in the three-sector scheme, but its share is small, only about 3% of GVA (2016-17).

=== NOTES 3:2 | Services Sector – Growth, Composition and Contribution
## SUB 0 | Services Exports, FDI and Digital Services
[2:0:0] # Exports
[2:0:1] - India's share of world services trade: **2% (2005) → 4.3% (2024)**; older target: **4.2% of world services exports by 2022**.
[2:0:2] - **Digitally delivered services exports:** India's share of the world total about **6% in 2023** (Economic Survey 2023-24).
[2:0:3] - Rank: **8th**-largest services exporter (WTO, April 2025 data); also 8th in 2014.
[2:0:4] - 2024-25: exports **USD 387.5 bn** vs imports USD 198.7 bn → surplus; **software and business services = 77.8%** of services exports.
[2:0:5] - Target: services exports **USD 1 trillion** within the USD 2 trillion export goal by **2030**.
[2:0:6] # FDI
[2:0:7] - **1997-2000:** largest FDI share – **services sector** in most books' keys (UPSC 2001; some keys say engineering).
[2:0:8] - **Last decade (to 2006):** services sector attracted the highest FDI among chemicals, food processing and telecom; April 2000-December 2025 cumulative: **services 16.38%**, computer software and hardware 15.63%, trading, telecom, automobiles.
[2:0:9] - FY2024-25: **services sector** again the **top** FDI equity recipient (~19%, about $9.35 bn), ahead of computer software and hardware (16%).
[2:0:10] - Large foreign investment came into **life insurance, banking and automobiles** (UPPCS 2009).
[2:0:11] ~~ Sources: Economic Survey 2023-24 ch.9 (digitally delivered services 6%); DPIIT FDI Factsheet Dec 2025; app notes 3:2 sub 3
## SUB 1 | Andhra Pradesh and the States
[2:1:0] - **AP:** services = **AP's largest sector** – about **43%** of GVA (current prices) (APPSC DL 2020).
[2:1:1] - Yet AP is **basically an agricultural economy** (APPSC Panchayat Secretary): agriculture is its main employer, so the success of its Five Year Plans depended mainly on **agriculture** (APPSC G2 2011).
[2:1:2] - AP 2025-26: services **Rs 3,59,931 crore (+10.67%)**; **communications** fastest (**15.13%**).
[2:1:3] - **Villaments** (villa + apartment) = new urban-housing trend in AP real estate (APPSC G2 Mains 2019).
[2:1:4] - Kerala **64.3%**, **Bihar 58.7%** of GSVA from services; Karnataka, Maharashtra, Tamil Nadu, Telangana = about **40%** of India's services output.
[2:1:5] - Largest services share in State GDP, 2008-09: **Delhi** > Chandigarh > Kerala > Maharashtra; 2023-24 (NITI Aayog, Oct 2025): Chandigarh 88.8%, Delhi 84.1%.
[2:1:6] ~~ Sources: AP Socio-Economic Survey 2024-25 (GVA by sector); NITI Aayog "India's Services Sector" (Oct 2025); app notes 3:2 sub 6
## SUB 2 | Share in GVA, Growth and Jobs – the Mismatch
[2:2:0] # Share of output
[2:2:1] - **Services = more than 50% of GVA** (Economic Survey 2025-26) – the **highest** contributor to GDP (WBCS 2017: "more than **50%**").
[2:2:2] TABLE head: Sector (2021-22) | Share of GDP | Share of workforce
  [2:2:2.0] Secondary | 24.29% | 22.24%
  [2:2:2.1] Tertiary | 53.89% | 33.80%
[2:2:3] - **Employment-output paradox** (**NITI Aayog**): services give over half of output but only about **one-third** of jobs; high-end services (IT, finance) need high skills, so most new jobs are in low-value trade, hospitality, transport.
[2:2:4] - **Regular wages: 51.5%** of service workers vs about 1% in agriculture.
[2:2:5] # Share of growth
[2:2:6] - **Contribution of services to GVA growth, 2011-12 to 2015-16: about 69%** (CGPSC 2017 key); in 2015-16 alone **66.1%** (Economic Survey 2015-16); 2017-18 about 72.5%.
[2:2:7] - Growth: **9.1%** in FY2025-26 (FAE; new series 8.9%); **10%** in Q1 FY2026-27, led by finance, real estate, IT and professional services (**12.1%**).
[2:2:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 14 p.4-5; Economic Survey 2015-16 Vol.2 ch.7 (services 66.1% of GVA growth); Economic Survey 2025-26; app notes 3:2 sub 1
## SUB 3 | What Is (and Is Not) a Service
[2:3:0] TABLE head: Tertiary (services) | Not services
  [2:3:0.0] Trade, hotels, restaurants | Mining & quarrying (primary)
  [2:3:0.1] Transport, storage, communication | Forestry, fishing (primary)
  [2:3:0.2] Finance, insurance, real estate, business services | Manufacturing, construction, utilities (secondary)
  [2:3:0.3] Public administration, defence, other services | Agriculture (primary)
[2:3:1] - **Construction is secondary**, not a service (CDS 2015); transport, hotels and restaurants and insurance are tertiary.
[2:3:2] - **NIC** lists **15** types of services; **WTO** uses the UN's **UNCPC** (Central Product Classification); AP Survey uses **8** service sub-sectors.
[2:3:3] TABLE head: Constitution list | Services
  [2:3:3.0] Union | Telecom, postal, broadcasting, financial services, insurance
  [2:3:3.1] State | Health, real estate
  [2:3:3.2] Concurrent | Education, accounting, engineering, electricity
[2:3:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.36 (NIC 15 types; three Lists); NCERT Class 10 Economics ch.2; app notes 3:2 sub 0
## SUB 4 | Service-led Growth and the 1991 Reforms
[2:4:0] - Boom began in the **mid-1980s**; sped up after the **1990s reforms** – services gained most from globalisation.
[2:4:1] - The 1991 reforms **did not bring much benefit to agriculture** (public investment in agriculture fell; APPSC ASO 2017) – unlike services, stock markets and power.
[2:4:2] - **Software** is a major export, but India **lacks a strong hardware base** (UPSC 1999: A true, R false).
[2:4:3] - **EAC-PM Chairman** (Nov 2025): service-led growth cannot last without a **manufacturing base**; manufacturing creates demand for logistics, IT, finance and steadies GDP cycles.
[2:4:4] - **Paradox of thrift** – popularised by **Keynes**.
[2:4:5] ~~ Sources: NCERT Class 11 Indian Economic Development ch.3 (reforms and agriculture); NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 14 p.9; app notes 3:2 sub 2
## SUB 5 | Indices and Terms
[2:5:0] TABLE head: Term | Meaning
  [2:5:0.0] PMI | Survey of purchasing managers; indicator of the health of the manufacturing (and services) sector; above 50 = expansion, below 50 = contraction
  [2:5:0.1] Index of Services Production (ISP) | MoSPI's first trial (for April 2026) – services counterpart of the IIP; monthly, within 60 days; base 2024-25; Laspeyres index
  [2:5:0.2] GCC | Offshore unit of an MNC doing its own core work (not a third-party vendor); over 1,800 in India
[2:5:1] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 02 p.34 (PMI); app notes 3:2 sub 4
## SUB 6 | GATS Modes of Supply
[2:6:0] TABLE head: Mode | Meaning | Example
  [2:6:0.0] Mode 1 | Cross-border supply | Software sent online
  [2:6:0.1] Mode 2 | Consumption abroad | Medical tourism
  [2:6:0.2] Mode 3 | Commercial presence | Foreign branch/subsidiary
  [2:6:0.3] Mode 4 | Presence of natural persons | IT professionals on visas
[2:6:1] ~~ Sources: WTO GATS Article I; app notes 3:2 sub 5

=== NOTES 3:3 | Role of the It and Ites Industry in India's Development
## SUB 0 | Firms, Exports and Destinations
[3:0:0] TABLE head: Item | Fact
  [3:0:0.0] Largest software company | TCS (largest exporter in 2002-03 and by market cap), then Infosys, Wipro
  [3:0:0.1] First Indian company on NASDAQ | Infosys (March 1999)
  [3:0:0.2] Biggest tech employers (2018) | Large Indian IT services firms (TCS, Infosys) – not Oracle, Apple or Microsoft
  [3:0:0.3] Destinations | USA and Europe; 2022-23: USA first, UK second (US and Canada 56%, Europe 30.8%, half of it the UK)
  [3:0:0.4] Major hubs (2012) | Bengaluru, Chennai, Hyderabad, Mumbai, Pune, NCR = ~90%
  [3:0:0.5] Export concentration | Seven cities = 70%; Bengaluru 38%
  [3:0:0.6] Tier-2/3 | Coimbatore, Indore, Jaipur, Bhubaneswar
  [3:0:0.7] AP | IT Park, Madhurawada (Rushikonda), Visakhapatnam – AP IT & GCC Policy 4.0
[3:0:1] - **Services (IT and ITES) benefited most from globalisation** after 1991 – outsourcing, FDI, global Indian firms.
[3:0:2] ~~ Sources: NASSCOM Strategic Review 2003 (TCS largest exporter 2002-03); RBI Survey on Computer Software & ITES Exports 2022-23 (Oct 2023); Infosys history (NASDAQ listing 1999); app notes 3:3 sub 3
[3:0:3] - **Company : work:** **Genpact** = business-process management and IT services (ex-GE Capital International Services), **not power generation**; Sasken = communication technology; Adobe India = software; Synthite = spice extracts and exports.
## SUB 1 | Revenue and Export Growth
[3:1:0] - 1990s boom: software exports grew **over 50% a year after 1995-96** because Indian firms were **cost-effective with international quality** (UPSC 2001: A and R true, R explains A); IX Plan (1997-2002) CAGR **52%** (exports 57%).
[3:1:1] - Question-period data (UPSC 2003): exports CAGR about **60%** over five years ✓; software and services growth about **28%** in rupee terms in 2001-02 ✓ → **both**.
[3:1:2] - Software services exports **2022-23: US $185.5 bn** (RBI, +18.4%); computer services 67.3%, ITES/BPO 32.7%.
[3:1:3] - **FY2025 (NASSCOM):** revenue **USD 282.6 bn** (+5.1%); exports **USD 224.4 bn** (~**79%**); domestic USD 58.2 bn.
[3:1:4] - Software/computer services = India's **largest services export** and **largest services forex earner** → offsets the **merchandise trade deficit**.
[3:1:5] ~~ Sources: Economic Survey 2002-03 (software exports); RBI Bulletin Oct 2023; NASSCOM Strategic Review 2025; app notes 3:3 sub 1
[3:1:6] - **Software and services exports 2012-13:** about **Rs 4.1 lakh crore** (**Rs 4,10,836 crore**, about USD 76 bn).
## SUB 2 | Origins – Policies and Institutions
[3:2:0] TABLE head: Policy / institution | Year | Role
  [3:2:0.0] Department of Electronics | 1970 | Foresaw India's export potential in software as early as the mid-1970s
  [3:2:0.1] Software Export Scheme | 1972 | Computers imported against an export obligation
  [3:2:0.2] Computer Policy / Software Export Policy | 1984 / 1986 | Liberalised imports and data links
  [3:2:0.3] NASSCOM | 1988 | Industry body; annual Strategic Review = data source
  [3:2:0.4] STPI | 1991 | Under the Department of Electronics; satellite data links + single-window clearances; India's first export-park model for software
  [3:2:0.5] NPIT 2012 | 2012 | Targets for 2020
  [3:2:0.6] MeitY | separate ministry 2016 | Earlier DeitY
[3:2:1] - **NPIT 2012** targets (2020): revenue USD 100 bn → 300 bn; exports USD 69 bn → **200 bn**; 10 million more skilled workers; **one e-literate person in every household** (not two).
[3:2:2] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 22 p.2 (software technology parks); app notes 3:3 sub 0
## SUB 3 | Employment
[3:3:0] - **~58 lakh (5.8 million)** direct jobs (FY2025); mostly **organised** jobs with contracts and social security; high share of women.
[3:3:1] - **Quarterly Employment Survey (QES)** – **Labour Bureau**; **9 non-farm sectors**; in 2021 rounds **manufacturing** had the largest share (~39-41%); IT/BPO ~7% but fastest growth (152%).
[3:3:2] ~~ Sources: NASSCOM Strategic Review 2025; Labour Bureau QES 2021; app notes 3:3 sub 2
[3:3:3] - **Dec 2014 to Dec 2015 (Labour Bureau QES, eight sectors):** **IT/BPO** contributed the most to the overall rise in employment.
## SUB 4 | Challenges and Role in Development
[3:4:0] TABLE head: Factor | Effect
  [3:4:0.0] External dependence | Visa rules, protectionism, client spending cycles
  [3:4:0.1] Automation | Routine ITES/BPM most exposed to generative AI
  [3:4:0.2] Jobs paradox | Fast GVA growth, few jobs – skill-intensive and automated
  [3:4:0.3] Other risks | Urban concentration, Bengaluru water stress, Chennai floods 2015, digital divide
[3:4:1] - IT **earns forex and formal jobs** but serves a **skilled few in a few cities** → cannot deliver broad development alone; a forex shortage is **not** one of its problems.
[3:4:2] - Skill gap: employers want AI-ready skills – cloud, GenAI, data analytics (May 2026).
[3:4:3] ~~ Sources: app notes 3:3 sub 4
## SUB 5 | Digital India and Data Protection
[3:5:0] - **Digital India**: launched **1 July 2015** (MeitY); **nine pillars**, first = **Broadband Highways**.
[3:5:1] - **BharatNet**: renamed from **NOFN** (2015); optical fibre to about 2.5 lakh Gram Panchayats.
[3:5:2] - **IndiaAI Mission**: Cabinet approval March 2024, outlay **Rs 10,372 crore**.
[3:5:3] - **DPDP Act 2023**: assent **11 August 2023**; **Data Protection Board of India** imposes penalties up to **Rs 250 crore**; **DPDP Rules 2025** notified November 2025.
[3:5:4] ~~ Sources: MeitY (digitalindia.gov.in); PIB 7 Mar 2024 (IndiaAI); DPDP Act 2023; app notes 3:3 sub 5

  x: The Software Export Scheme of 1972 permitted computers to be imported against an export obligation.

  x: The Computer Policy came in 1984 and the Software Export Policy in 1986; both liberalised imports and data links.

  x: High-end services record fast GVA growth but limited job creation because they are skill-intensive and automated.

=== NOTES 3:4 | Other Services – Tourism, Banking, Insurance, Health and Education
## SUB 0 | Insurance – Milestones, Companies and Regulator
[4:0:0] TABLE head: Act / body | Year | Provision
  [4:0:0.0] LIC Act | 1 September 1956 | LIC formed; life insurance nationalised (over 240 insurers merged)
  [4:0:0.1] GIC | 1972 | General insurers nationalised; National, New India, Oriental, United India became GIC subsidiaries
  [4:0:0.2] Malhotra Committee | 1993-94 | Open insurance to private companies + independent regulator (not the Gadgil Committee)
  [4:0:0.3] IRDA Act | 1999 | Set up IRDAI – statutory regulator, HQ Hyderabad; set up April 2000
  [4:0:0.4] Agriculture Insurance Company of India (AIC) | 2002 | Implements crop insurance (RKBY/NAIS) in AP and other States; before it the GIC ran crop insurance
  [4:0:0.5] PMFBY | 2016 | Replaced NAIS (Rashtriya Krishi Bima Yojana) and MNAIS
  [4:0:0.6] Sabka Bima Sabki Raksha (Amendment of Insurance Laws) Act | Passed 17 Dec 2025 | FDI cap 74% → 100%
[4:0:1] - **LIC is not the oldest insurer** (Oriental Life 1818; National Insurance 1906); **National Insurance** was nationalised in 1972 and made a GIC subsidiary; **United India** HQ = **Chennai** (UPSC 2006 → B and C).
[4:0:2] - The **2025 Act** amends the Insurance Act 1938, LIC Act 1956 and IRDA Act 1999 and creates a Policyholders' Education and Protection Fund.
[4:0:3] - IRDAI vision: **"Insurance for All by 2047"**; FY25 premium income **Rs 11.9 lakh crore**; **health** passed **motor** as the top non-life segment.
[4:0:4] - IRDAI panel on a standard **cyber liability** insurance product (Oct 2020): chaired by **P. Umesh**.
[4:0:5] - Only about **5%** of Indians had insurance cover in the 2000s (APPSC G1 2008 key).
[4:0:6] - AP enrolment under PMFBY, 2016-17: about **15 lakh** farmers (APPSC ASO 2017 key).
[4:0:7] ~~ Sources: Rocket Indian Economy #30 p.44 (LIC 1 Sept 1956); NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.32 (IRDA Act 1999); GIC Re history (gicre.in); aicofindia.com (AIC 2002, RKBY); app notes 3:4 sub 0
[4:0:8] - **Micro Bachat** (2019) = **LIC**'s micro-insurance savings plan.
## SUB 1 | Transport, Postal and Telecom Services
[4:1:0] TABLE head: Service | Date / fact
  [4:1:0.0] UDAN (Ude Desh ka Aam Nagrik) | 2016; Regional Connectivity Scheme of the Ministry of Civil Aviation; first flight Shimla-Delhi, April 2017 (not 2015); tier II and III cities
  [4:1:0.1] BSNL | Corporatised 1 October 2000 from the Department of Telecom Services
  [4:1:0.2] India Post Payments Bank | 2018; India Post got the RBI payments-bank licence (2015)
  [4:1:0.3] India Post – TCS | Pact to digitally reimagine postal services
  [4:1:0.4] 5G | Began 1 October 2022
  [4:1:0.5] Mobile phones (2000) | India 4 per 1,000 people vs world average 121 (UNDP HDR 2002)
[4:1:1] - India Post: about **1.64 lakh** post offices – **largest postal network in the world**, ~90% rural; Union Budget 2022-23: **100%** of 1.5 lakh post offices on core banking.
[4:1:2] - Overall tele-density: **91.74%** (quarter ending Dec 2025, TRAI); AP banking & insurance grew 13.59% (AP SES 2025-26).
[4:1:3] - **e-banking** = **electronic** banking.
[4:1:4] ~~ Sources: Ministry of Civil Aviation (UDAN); RBI press release 19 Aug 2015 (payments bank licences); UNDP HDR 2002 Table 12; TRAI; app notes 3:4 sub 6
## SUB 2 | Insurance Terms and Principles
[4:2:0] TABLE head: Term | Meaning
  [4:2:0.0] Insurance penetration | Total premium as % of GDP – not "insured persons per 100 people" (APPSC G2 2017 → none of these)
  [4:2:0.1] Insurance density | Premium per capita (USD 97 in FY25)
  [4:2:0.2] Principle of indemnity | Restores the insured to the pre-loss position, no profit; applies to fire, marine, motor, health – not life insurance
  [4:2:0.3] Actuary | Insurance expert who judges risk and estimates the premium
  [4:2:0.4] Cyber insurance | Data breach, hacking, ransomware, identity theft
  [4:2:0.5] Aviation hull | Physical damage to the aircraft itself
  [4:2:0.6] P&I | Shipowners' third-party liabilities; first sovereign-backed cover: Bharat Maritime Insurance Pool (Jul 2026)
[4:2:1] ~~ Sources: Economic Survey 2025-26 Summary - Part 1 p.33-34 (penetration, density); NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 01 p.33; app notes 3:4 sub 1
[4:2:2] - **Insurance** is the measure against risk – **life insurance** against life insecurity; **insurable** risks are pure and measurable (**accident**, fire, death, theft), **non-insurable** ones speculative (business, market or gambling loss).
[4:2:3] - **Cyber insurance for individuals (UPSC 2020):** besides loss of funds it covers **restoring the computer after malware**, a **consultant's cost in cyber extortion** and **legal defence against third-party suits** – not a new computer after wilful damage → **A, C and D**.
## SUB 3 | Tourism
[4:3:0] TABLE head: Item | Fact
  [4:3:0.0] ITDC | October 1966; prime mover of the tourism and hotel industry
  [4:3:0.1] Swadesh Darshan | 2014-15; integrated thematic circuits; revamped as Swadesh Darshan 2.0
  [4:3:0.2] PRASHAD | 2014-15; Pilgrimage Rejuvenation and Spiritual, Heritage Augmentation Drive
  [4:3:0.3] TTCI → TTDI | Published by the World Economic Forum (TTDI from 2021); India 39th of 119 (2024)
  [4:3:0.4] NIDHI | National Integrated Database of Hospitality Industry (Tourism Ministry)
  [4:3:0.5] Vision 2047 | USD 1 trillion tourism economy, 100 million foreign tourist arrivals
  [4:3:0.6] National Strategy for Sustainable Tourism | Ministry of Tourism, June 2022; Responsible Traveller campaign
[4:3:1] - Top State in **foreign** tourist visits: **Maharashtra** (2010, 2023, 2024); top in **domestic** visits: **Uttar Pradesh**.
[4:3:2] - Foreign tourist spending in India = **export of services** = **invisible export** (foreign visitors at the **XIX Commonwealth Games**, UPSC 2011).
[4:3:3] - Invisible exports: IT/software, tourism, banking, insurance, financial services, advertising.
[4:3:4] - **Sustainable tourism** = managing tourism and the environment while maintaining cultural integrity and ecological processes.
[4:3:5] - Economic Survey 2023-24: NIDHI, the 2047 vision, rising hotel occupancy and an expanding **Services PMI** – **all** four statements valid (APPSC G1).
[4:3:6] ~~ Sources: Ministry of Tourism, India Tourism Data Compendium 2025; Economic Survey 2023-24 ch.9; WEF TTDI 2024; app notes 3:4 sub 3
## SUB 4 | Health Services
[4:4:0] TABLE head: NHA edition | Government health spending
  [4:4:0.0] 2018-19 | 1.28% of GDP
  [4:4:0.1] Latest (Sep 2026) | 1.43% of GDP vs NHP 2017 target 2.5%
[4:4:1] - Health share of total government (Centre + States) spending: 6.12% (2021-22) → **4.89%** (2022-23).
[4:4:2] - **PM-JAY (2018)**: **Rs 5 lakh** per family; with State schemes covers ~**80%** of citizens.
[4:4:3] - **Aarogyasri:** launched **1 April 2007** in undivided AP by the **Y.S. Rajasekhara Reddy** government; named after **Rajiv Gandhi**; **cashless** cover for catastrophic illness of **BPL** families; continued as Telangana's flagship scheme after 2014.
[4:4:4] - State + central schemes cover **70%** of AP households (2026).
[4:4:5] ~~ Sources: National Health Accounts estimates (MoHFW); aarogyasri.telangana.gov.in; app notes 3:4 sub 4
## SUB 5 | Para-banking
[4:5:0] - Legal basis: **Section 6, Banking Regulation Act, 1949**, with **RBI** approval; run **departmentally or through subsidiaries** (subsidiary route: **Section 19(1)**).
[4:5:1] - Para-banking = **credit cards, insurance** (as **corporate agent** or **joint venture**), **factoring**, equipment leasing, mutual funds – **not** "credit to paramilitary personnel" or issuing currency.
[4:5:2] - Bank credit-deposit ratio: 62-year high of 82.6% in Q1 FY27 (Jul 2026).
[4:5:3] ~~ Sources: RBI Master Circular – Para-banking Activities; app notes 3:4 sub 2
## SUB 6 | Education Services
[4:6:0] - Education is on the **Concurrent List**; **NEP 2020** target: public spending on education = **6%** of GDP.
[4:6:1] ~~ Sources: Constitution Seventh Schedule List III entry 25; NEP 2020 para 26.1; app notes 3:4 sub 5

[nf9e8ef808e1] sub2
  Which one of the following correctly distinguishes insurance density from insurance penetration?
  Insurance density is total premium as a percentage of GDP, whereas insurance penetration is premium per capita | Insurance density refers to claims settled, whereas insurance penetration refers to policies issued | *Insurance density is premium per capita, whereas insurance penetration is total premium as a percentage of GDP | Both terms refer to the number of insured persons per 1,000 population
  x: Insurance density is measured as premium per capita, generally in US dollars. Insurance penetration is the total premium as a percentage of GDP; neither measure means insured persons per 100 or 1,000 people.
[n958a06406fa] sub2
  What is the main aim of the principle of indemnity in insurance?
  Provide the insured with a profit after occurrence of loss | *Restore the insured to the pre-loss position without allowing profit | Cover only the death of the insured | Guarantee payment irrespective of the occurrence of loss
  x: The principle of indemnity restores the insured to the position existing before the loss and does not permit profit from the insurance claim.

  x: Aviation hull insurance covers physical damage to the aircraft; P&I covers shipowners' liabilities and cyber insurance covers ransomware.

=== NOTES 3:5 | Occupational Structure of India – Trends and Inter-state Variation
## SUB 0 | Census – History and Worker Data
[5:0:0] # History
[5:0:1] - First Census of the modern (non-synchronous) system: **1872** under Lord **Mayo** – the Viceroy who arranged the first Census; first **regular**, synchronous and scientific Census: **1881** under Lord **Ripon**.
[5:0:2] - **Census 2011** = **15th** since 1872, **7th** after Independence; covered **6,40,867 villages**. **Census 2027** = first **app-based digital** Census.
[5:0:3] # Worker categories
[5:0:4] TABLE head: Category | Meaning
  [5:0:4.0] Main worker | Worked 6 months or more in the reference year
  [5:0:4.1] Marginal worker | Worked less than 6 months
  [5:0:4.2] Cultivator | Works on own/leased land
  [5:0:4.3] Agricultural labourer | Works on another person's land for wages
  [5:0:4.4] Household industry | Production in the household, by family members
  [5:0:4.5] Other workers | All other work – services, trade, factories
[5:0:5] # Census 2011 numbers
[5:0:6] - Total workers **48.19 crore** (481.9 million, not 382 million); **main workers 36.26 crore**; male workers about **332 million**.
[5:0:7] - **Work participation rate 39.8%** (39.79%) – not 39.1, 42.3 or 43.6.
[5:0:8] - Census 2001: AP's female WPR **35.1%**; rural female WPR in AP about **43.3%** (APPSC G2 2011 key); mining and quarrying employed about **0.6%** of workers.
[5:0:9] ~~ Sources: Census of India 2011, Primary Census Abstract – Data Highlights (censusindia.gov.in); Census 2001 Final Population Totals; app notes 3:5 sub 2
[5:0:10] - **Census 2001:** workers = **39.1%** of the population (about 402 million; provisional **39.26%**, i.e. **39.3%** in keys); Census 2011 39.8%.
## SUB 1 | Workforce Share by Sector Over Time
[5:1:0] TABLE head: Year / survey | Agriculture share of workers
  [5:1:0.0] 1951 | 72%
  [5:1:0.1] Census 2001 | 58.2% (labour force in agriculture 56.7%)
  [5:1:0.2] NSS 2011-12 | 48.9%
  [5:1:0.3] Census 2011 | 54.6%
  [5:1:0.4] PLFS 2023-24 | 46.1%
  [5:1:0.5] PLFS Q2 FY26 | 42.4%
[5:1:1] - **Agriculture** = always the **highest employment share** (falling); the main livelihood **on the eve of Independence**. Direct employment in agriculture: about **55%** of the labour force (Census 2011), so 20, 30, 40 and 65% are all off (UPPCS Mains 2012).
[5:1:2] - Now: agriculture about **18% of GVA** but **42.4%** of the workforce.
[5:1:3] - 2021-22 workforce: primary 43.96%, secondary 22.24%, tertiary **33.80%**; urban employment: services **62.0%** (Q2 FY26).
[5:1:4] - PLFS Jan-Mar 2026: rural agricultural employment 58.5% → 55.8%; workforce **61.6 crore** (PLFS 2025).
[5:1:5] - **Agricultural households = 57.8%** of rural households (NSSO 70th round, 2013).
[5:1:6] - **NSS 2011-12: self-employed** = largest category of workers (about 52%), then casual labour, then regular salaried.
[5:1:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 22 p.12 (occupational structure); NSS 68th round Report 554 (2011-12); PLFS Annual Report 2023-24; app notes 3:5 sub 1
## SUB 2 | Informal and Unorganised Employment
[5:2:0] TABLE head: Term | Meaning
  [5:2:0.0] Unorganised sector (general) | Small and scattered units largely outside the control of the government; low pay, no job security (APPSC GS, SSC 2017)
  [5:2:0.1] Unorganised sector (NCEUS) | Unincorporated private enterprises of individuals/households, fewer than 10 workers
  [5:2:0.2] Organised manufacturing | Registered factories – 10+ workers with power / 20+ without
  [5:2:0.3] Informal worker (NCEUS) | Unorganised-sector workers + organised-sector workers without social security
  [5:2:0.4] Informalisation of formal enterprises | Contract and outsourced workers inside formal firms
  [5:2:0.5] "Formal informality" | New EPFO 'formal' jobs that are short-term, low-wage or contractual
[5:2:1] - **NCEUS**: chaired by **Dr. Arjun Sengupta**; formed **20.09.2004**, term ended 30.04.2009; found **77%** of Indians lived on less than **Rs 20 a day**.
[5:2:2] - Informal employment: **over 80%** of the workforce (Mar 2026); non-farm informal share about **78%** (PLFS 2022-23).
[5:2:3] - **NSS 73rd round (2015-16)**: unincorporated non-agricultural enterprises employed about **11 crore**.
[5:2:4] - **e-Shram**: **31 crore+** registered; **four Labour Codes** merged **29** laws (Code on Wages **2019**; others 2020).
[5:2:5] - Social-protection coverage: 19% (2015) → **64.3%** (2025) – India 2nd after China.
[5:2:6] - Direct factory employment 61% → **47%** (2011-2023); contract workers = 42%; small and marginal farmers ~**86%**.
[5:2:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 17 p.7 (Arjun Sengupta, Rs 20 a day); NCEUS Report 2007; NCERT Class 10 Economics ch.2; app notes 3:5 sub 6
## SUB 3 | Meaning and Theory of Occupational Structure
[5:3:0] - **Occupational structure** = **distribution of the working population among different occupations** – primary, secondary and tertiary.
[5:3:1] - **Clark-Fisher thesis**: as income rises, labour shifts primary → secondary → tertiary; India moved from agriculture **directly to services** with no manufacturing-led stage.
[5:3:2] - **Structural retardation**: the occupational structure changed far more slowly than output – because **investment was directed towards capital-intensive industries** (UPSC 1995).
[5:3:3] - **Premature deindustrialisation**: manufacturing fails to absorb enough farm labour.
[5:3:4] - An **increase in the working population** is **not** an adverse effect of population growth (smaller holdings, unemployment, less food per head are).
[5:3:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 03 p.21 (occupational structure); app notes 3:5 sub 0
## SUB 4 | Rural Workers and Women Workers
[5:4:0] - Census 2011: **72.4%** of workers in **rural** areas, against **68.8%** of population.
[5:4:1] - **Feminisation of agriculture:** female workers **55%** agricultural labourers, 24% cultivators; women hold only **12.8%** of operational holdings; driven by **subsistence farming, unpaid family work, distress employment** (not formal manufacturing jobs).
[5:4:2] - Rural women self-employed: 60% (2011-12) → **73%** (2023-24); UN: **2026 = International Year of the Woman Farmer**.
[5:4:3] - Female LFPR (15+): 23.3% (2017-18) → **30%** (2019-20) → **40%** (2025); rural 33.0% → **45.9%**; women = **32.5%** of employment (2025).
[5:4:4] - Labour participation of women about **32%** (APPSC AEE 2012 key, ILO estimate); Census 2011 female WPR 25.5%.
[5:4:5] ~~ Sources: Census of India 2011; PLFS Annual Reports 2017-18 to 2023-24; app notes 3:5 sub 4
## SUB 5 | Cultivators vs Agricultural Labourers
[5:5:0] TABLE head: Category | Census 2001 | Census 2011 | Share 2011
  [5:5:0.0] Cultivators | 127.3 million | 118.8 million (11.87 crore) ↓ | 24.7%
  [5:5:0.1] Agricultural labourers | 106.8 million | 144.3 million (14.43 crore) ↑ | 30.0%
[5:5:1] - **2011** = first Census in which **labourers outnumbered cultivators**.
[5:5:2] - Cause – **fragmentation**: average holding **2.28 ha (1971) → 1.08 ha (2015-16)**.
[5:5:3] ~~ Sources: Census of India 2011, Primary Census Abstract; Agriculture Census 2015-16; app notes 3:5 sub 3
## SUB 6 | Labour Indicators and Employment Status
[5:6:0] TABLE head: Indicator | Meaning
  [5:6:0.0] LFPR | % of population working or seeking/available for work
  [5:6:0.1] WPR | % of population employed
  [5:6:0.2] Unemployment rate | % of the labour force that is unemployed
  [5:6:0.3] Usual status (ps+ss) | Reference period 365 days
  [5:6:0.4] Current weekly status | Reference period 7 days – monthly/quarterly bulletins
[5:6:1] - Monthly PLFS from April 2025; July 2026 rural unemployment 5% → **4.5%**.
[5:6:2] - PLFS 2025: regular wage/salaried 22% → **24%**; **self-employment 58% → 56%**.
[5:6:3] - **India Employment Report 2024:** March 2024, **IHD with the ILO**; focus on **youth employment, education and skills**.
[5:6:4] ~~ Sources: PLFS methodology (MoSPI); ILO-IHD India Employment Report 2024; app notes 3:5 sub 5
## SUB 7 | Jobless Growth and the Slow Shift Out of Agriculture
[5:7:0] - **Jobless growth**: output grows without a matching rise in quality employment – **late 1990s**.
[5:7:1] - **Employment elasticity:** **negative in agriculture**; **construction** and services most employment-intensive (RBI study, 2014).
[5:7:2] TABLE head: Factor | Effect
  [5:7:2.0] History | Colonial deindustrialisation pushed artisans onto land
  [5:7:2.1] Capital intensity | Growth failed to absorb surplus farm labour
  [5:7:2.2] Skills | Only 4.2% had formal vocational training
  [5:7:2.3] Job deficit | ~20 million entrants a year vs ~2 million new jobs
[5:7:3] ~~ Sources: RBI study on employment elasticity (2014); app notes 3:5 sub 8
## SUB 8 | Gig and Platform Work
[5:8:0] - **Gig worker**: earns outside the traditional employer-employee relationship; defined **for the first time** in the **Code on Social Security, 2020**.
[5:8:1] - **Platform work**: uses an **online platform** to reach others or provide services; gig workers = about **2%** of the 61.6 crore workforce; **not yet a separate PLFS category**.
[5:8:2] ~~ Sources: Code on Social Security 2020 s.2(35), 2(61); app notes 3:5 sub 7
## SUB 9 | Types of Unemployment
[5:9:0] TABLE head: Type | Feature | Linked with
  [5:9:0.0] Disguised | More people than needed; marginal productivity near zero | Agriculture
  [5:9:0.1] Seasonal | Work only in sowing/harvest | Agriculture
  [5:9:0.2] Structural | Skill-job mismatch (new technology) | Industry, educated
  [5:9:0.3] Frictional | Short gap between jobs | Urban/organised
  [5:9:0.4] Cyclical | Demand falls in a business-cycle slump | Industry
[5:9:1] ~~ Sources: NCERT Class 11 Indian Economic Development ch.7; app notes 3:5 sub 9
## SUB 10 | Inter-state Variation and Andhra Pradesh
[5:10:0] TABLE head: Census 2011 | Andhra Pradesh (13 districts) | India
  [5:10:0.0] Cultivators | 14.5% | 24.7%
  [5:10:0.1] Agricultural labourers | 47.9% | 30.0%
  [5:10:0.2] All agriculture | 62.4% | 54.6%
[5:10:1] - AP labourers : cultivators about **3:1** (India about 1.2:1).
[5:10:2] - High **"other workers"** (services): urbanised **Delhi, Chandigarh, Puducherry**; highest share of cultivators: **Himachal Pradesh** (57.9%); of agricultural labourers: **Bihar** (52.8%).
[5:10:3] - AP (PLFS 2023-24): agriculture **45.6%** (India 46.1%); youth (15-29) unemployment **17.5%**; rural casual labour **AP 33.8% vs India 22.5%**.
[5:10:4] - 2026 rural female LFPR gains: **West Bengal, UP, Bihar**.
[5:10:5] ~~ Sources: Census 2011 PCA (AP); PLFS Annual Report 2023-24; app notes 3:5 sub 10
