=== NOTES 3:6 | Economic Development Since Independence – Phases, Growth Rates and the Hindu Rate of Growth
## SUB 0 | Phases and Growth Rates
[6:0:0] TABLE head: Phase | Average growth
  [6:0:0.0] Before Independence | about 1% a year; agriculture 0.3%; agriculture the main livelihood on the eve of Independence
  [6:0:0.1] Plan era (Plans 1-11) | about 4.5%; agriculture 2-3%, industry 6-8%
  [6:0:0.2] 1950-1980 ("Hindu rate") | ~3.5%
  [6:0:0.3] 1980-81 to 1989-90 | 5.13-5.18%
  [6:0:0.4] Post-reform | ~6.5%; peak 8.5% in 2003-08
  [6:0:0.5] Post-Covid (2022-23 series) | 7.2% (2023-24), 7.1% (2024-25), 7.7% (2025-26 PE)
[6:0:1] - **Expected growth 2012-13** (Economic Survey 2011-12 / Budget 2012-13): **7.6%** (±0.25); 2008-09 expected about **9%** (actual 6.7%).
[6:0:2] - UPSC 2011: GDP and per capita growth did **not** rise steadily in 2006-11 (growth fell from 9.6% in 2006-07 to about 6.7% in 2008-09 and then recovered) → **neither**.
[6:0:3] - **High growth** depends on savings and their **optimal allocation** (APPSC G1 1990-91 key).
[6:0:4] - **Worst years:** **1979-80: -5.2%** (drought, oil shock); **2020-21: -5.8%** (Covid-19).
[6:0:5] - RBI outlook 2026-27: **6.6%** (Jun 2026); **Arvind Subramanian**: 2011-17 growth about **4.5%**, not 7%; AP **9.88%** vs India 7.4% (2025-26).
[6:0:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 12 p.11; Economic Survey 2011-12 ch.1 (7.6% for 2012-13); NSO; app notes 3:6 subs 1, 4-5
[6:0:7] - **A-R (around 2001):** growth was **not stagnant** (about 6% a year in the 1990s) but **foodgrain output stagnated** near 200 million tonnes for several years → **A false, R true**.
[6:0:8] - **Fastest-growing major economy:** in the 2000s India was **second after China**; since 2015-16 India has been the **fastest-growing major economy**.
## SUB 1 | Hindu Rate of Growth
[6:1:0] TABLE head: Particular | Fact
  [6:1:0.0] Coined by | Prof. Raj Krishna (not V.K.R.V. Rao, Mahalanobis, Amartya Sen)
  [6:1:0.1] Year | 1978 (some books: 1976)
  [6:1:0.2] Variable | Growth of national income / real GDP – not per capita income, population or money
  [6:1:0.3] Rate | About 3.5% a year (3.52%, 1950-1980) – not 5.5%
  [6:1:0.4] Period | 1950s to early 1980s (pre-reform planning era)
  [6:1:0.5] Per capita growth | Only 1.37% a year – barely above population growth
[6:1:1] - It describes **stagnant / low growth** of the Indian economy in 1950-80 – nothing to do with the Hindu population, faster growth or inequality.
[6:1:2] - Broken in the **1980s** (5.1-5.5%); 2026 echo: a **"Bharat rate of growth"** of 8%+ through AI-led "Reforms 3.0" (Jun 2026).
[6:1:3] ~~ Sources: Rocket Indian Economy p.98 (Hindu rate: Raj Krishna, national income/GDP, 3.5%, 1950s-early 1980s); NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 12 p.11 (3.52%, per capita 1.37%); app notes 3:6 sub 0
## SUB 2 | The 1991 Reforms – Trigger and Record
[6:2:0] - **Economic reforms (LPG / New Economic Policy): July 1991** – opened the economy to foreign and private investment.
[6:2:1] - **Trigger: balance-of-payments crisis** – reserves for only about **two weeks** of imports (under $1 bn); fiscal deficit **8.4%** (1990); the **Gulf War (1990-91)** raised oil prices and cut remittances.
[6:2:2] - Shortcomings: faster growth did not cut poverty enough, few quality jobs, unbalanced regional development; **rising consumer-goods imports is not usually listed** as a shortcoming.
[6:2:3] - 1997-98 slowdown – **East Asian financial crisis**; currency devaluation there was **unfavourable** for Indian exports.
[6:2:4] TABLE head: View | Argument
  [6:2:4.0] 1980s turn | Rodrik-Subramanian (2004): transition around 1980 from a pro-business (not pro-market) shift; DeLong (2003): take-off in the 1980s
  [6:2:4.1] 1991 turn | 1980s growth was debt-fuelled → 1991 crisis → sustained high growth after reforms
[6:2:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.32 (two weeks of imports, reforms 1991); NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 13 p.6-11 (LPG); app notes 3:6 subs 7-8
## SUB 3 | Chronology of Milestones
[6:3:0] TABLE head: Year | Event
  [6:3:0.0] 1938 | First National Planning Committee
  [6:3:0.1] 1 Jan 1949 | RBI nationalised
  [6:3:0.2] 1951 | First Five Year Plan; First Finance Commission (K.C. Neogy)
  [6:3:0.3] 1952 | Community Development Programme (2 October 1952)
  [6:3:0.4] 1955 | SBI and ICICI
  [6:3:0.5] 1960 | First Agricultural University – Pantnagar
  [6:3:0.6] 1964 | IDBI and UTI
  [6:3:0.7] 1966-69 | Plan Holiday – wars (1962, 1965) and droughts; rupee devalued 36.5% on 6 June 1966
  [6:3:0.8] July 1969 | Nationalisation of 14 banks
  [6:3:0.9] 1971 | Garibi Hatao; Dandekar-Rath poverty study
  [6:3:0.10] 1974 / 1975 | First KVK (Puducherry) / first RRB – Prathama Grameen Bank
  [6:3:0.11] 1977-78 | Food-for-Work; Antyodaya in Rajasthan
  [6:3:0.12] 1978-79 | IRDP introduced (trial); nationwide 1980
  [6:3:0.13] 1980 | Second bank nationalisation; NREP
  [6:3:0.14] 1982 | NABARD; DWCRA
  [6:3:0.15] 1990 | SIDBI; first Human Development Report (UNDP, Mahbub-ul-Haq)
  [6:3:0.16] 1993 | First private-sector mutual fund
[6:3:1] ~~ Sources: Rocket Indian Economy #30 p.44 (banking milestones); NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.12-14; app notes 3:6 subs 6-7
## SUB 4 | Policy Orientation 1947-1991 – Planning and Import Substitution
[6:4:0] TABLE head: Policy / plan | Year | Provision
  [6:4:0.0] First Industrial Policy | 1948 | IFCI set up
  [6:4:0.1] IPR 1956 ("Economic Constitution") | 1956 | Schedule A = 17 industries for the State; Schedule B = 12; rest private under licensing
  [6:4:0.2] Second Plan (Mahalanobis) | 1956-61 | Heavy industries; import substitution adopted as the strategy of industrialisation
  [6:4:0.3] Third Plan | 1961-66 | Continued import substitution (did not introduce it)
  [6:4:0.4] 1980s reforms | 1980s | Pro-business: broad-banding, MRTP limit raised to Rs 100 crore
  [6:4:0.5] 1991 reforms | 1991 | Reserved industries 17 → 8; delicensing
[6:4:1] - Major focus 1947-1991: **import substitution** with a large public sector – not export promotion, deregulation or less public investment.
[6:4:2] - UPSC 2009: Second Plan heavy industry ✓; "Third Plan introduced import substitution" ✗ → **a only**.
[6:4:3] - **Bombay Plan (1944)**: industrialists themselves sought **state protection** and planning support.
[6:4:4] ~~ Sources: NCERT Class 11 Indian Economic Development ch.2 (import substitution, first seven Plans); NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.25 (IPR 1956 "Economic Constitution"); app notes 3:6 sub 2
[6:4:5] - **Rajiv Gandhi (1980s):** telecom moved towards a **private-sector** orientation – private manufacture of equipment (1984), **C-DOT** (1984), **MTNL and VSNL** corporatised (1986).
## SUB 5 | Investment and Growth – ICOR
[6:5:0] - **ICOR** = change in capital ÷ change in output; **lower ICOR = more efficient** investment.
[6:5:1] - **Harrod-Domar** (First Plan): **g = s/v**; a higher capital-output ratio with the same savings → **lower growth**.
[6:5:2] - **Second Plan**: investment 7% → 11% of national income; **heavy, capital-intensive** industry (not light consumer goods) with long gestation → high ICOR.
[6:5:3] - GFCF about **30%** of GDP (2025-26).
[6:5:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 (plans); app notes 3:6 sub 3

=== NOTES 3:7 | Economic Planning in India – Origins, Planning Commission, National Development Council
## SUB 0 | Planning Commission – Set-up, Status, Chairman
[7:0:0] TABLE head: Particular | Fact
  [7:0:0.0] Set up | 15 March 1950 (began work 28 March 1950), with the Budget of 1950-51
  [7:0:0.1] How | Cabinet (executive) resolution of the Government of India – "a resolution of the Government of India"
  [7:0:0.2] Recommended by | Advisory Planning Board (1946), chaired by K.C. Neogy – not the Congress National Planning Committee
  [7:0:0.3] Basis | In line with Article 39 (DPSP) objectives
  [7:0:0.4] Status | Non-constitutional, non-statutory, advisory body (Rocket: also "quasi-political")
  [7:0:0.5] Chairman | Prime Minister, ex officio; first – Jawaharlal Nehru (as first PM)
  [7:0:0.6] Model | Broadly the Soviet Gosplan
  [7:0:0.7] Replaced by | NITI Aayog, 1 January 2015 (also a Cabinet resolution); last Plan = Twelfth (2012-17)
[7:0:1] - Body set up after Independence to prepare a **common plan** for the country = **Planning Commission**.
[7:0:2] - **Five-Year Plan idea in India: Jawaharlal Nehru** (as PM and first Chairman); planned development began with the **First Plan from 1 April 1951**.
[7:0:3] - **Functions:** assess material, capital and human resources; **formulate** plans; fix stages and priorities; determine resources; **appraise progress**; recommend on policy and administration; **perspective planning**.
[7:0:4] - **Execution is NOT its function** – plans were executed by ministries and States.
[7:0:5] - No mention in the Constitution; **no fixed tenure or qualifications** for the Deputy Chairman and members.
[7:0:6] - Was the **nodal agency for poverty estimation** at national and state level (now NITI Aayog, its successor).
[7:0:7] - **UIDAI** (Unique Identification Authority, 2009) began as an **attached office of the Planning Commission**; statutory since the Aadhaar Act 2016, under **MeitY**.
[7:0:8] - **National Planning Council (1965):** expert advisory body attached to the Commission.
[7:0:9] > 1950 = Planning Commission; 1952 = NDC; 2015 = NITI Aayog. "Advisory body" is the keyed status.
[7:0:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.4-5; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.28; Rocket Indian Economy #23 p.33; app notes 3:7 sub 1, 3:8 sub 1
## SUB 1 | National Development Council (NDC)
[7:1:0] TABLE head: Particular | Fact
  [7:1:0.0] Set up | 6 August 1952 – executive resolution, on the recommendation of the draft outline of the First Plan
  [7:1:0.1] Status | Neither constitutional nor statutory; extra-constitutional; advice not binding; to meet at least twice a year
  [7:1:0.2] Chairman | Prime Minister (presides over meetings)
  [7:1:0.3] Members | PM, all Union Cabinet Ministers (since 1967), Chief Ministers of all States, UT administrators, members of the Planning Commission
  [7:1:0.4] Secretary | Secretary of the Planning Commission
  [7:1:0.5] NOT members | President, Vice-President, Governors, Chairman of the Finance Commission, Secretary of the Ministry of Planning and Programme Implementation
[7:1:1] - **Objectives:** **chief – secure cooperation of the States in executing the plan** (NextGen); strengthen and mobilise the nation's efforts and resources for the plan; promote common economic policies; ensure **balanced and rapid development** of all regions.
[7:1:2] - **Functions:** prescribe **guidelines for the national plan, including assessment of resources**; consider and **approve the draft Five-Year Plan** – the **apex / final authority** that approves plans (can modify the draft).
[7:1:3] - **Sarkaria Commission:** constitutional status under **Article 263**, rename it "**National Economic and Development Council**".
[7:1:4] - Now inactive; its role passed to **NITI Aayog's Governing Council** (formal abolition pending).
[7:1:5] - **Chronology (UPPCS 2003):** India joins IMF (**Dec 1945**) → first devaluation (**Sept 1949**) → First Plan submitted to Parliament (**draft outline July 1951**) → NDC (**Aug 1952**).
[7:1:6] > NDC is NOT 1950 and NOT statutory; "guidelines incl. assessment of resources" = NDC, "assess resources and draft" = Planning Commission.
[7:1:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Polity - APPSC 20 (NDC: composition, objectives); NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.28, p.53 (NDC finally approves plans); app notes 3:7 sub 2
## SUB 2 | Origins – Pre-independence Plans
[7:2:0] TABLE head: Year | Effort | Person / idea
  [7:2:0.0] 1931 | Karachi Resolution (INC) | Economic freedom; state ownership / control of key industries
  [7:2:0.1] 1934 | Planned Economy for India | M. Visvesvaraya – first blueprint; ten-year plan to double national income; "Father of Indian Planning"; Bharat Ratna 1955
  [7:2:0.2] 1934 | FICCI | Called for national planning (Depression, New Deal, Soviet experiment)
  [7:2:0.3] 1938 | National Planning Committee (Congress) | Initiated by Subhas Chandra Bose (Haripura); chaired by Jawaharlal Nehru; final report 1949
  [7:2:0.4] 1944 | Bombay Plan | Eight industrialists (J.R.D. Tata, G.D. Birla, Kasturbhai Lalbhai, Purshottamdas Thakurdas, Ardeshir Dalal, John Mathai) – state-led industrialisation with protection
  [7:2:0.5] 1944 | Gandhian Plan | Shriman Narayan Agarwal (not Kripalani) – decentralised, self-contained villages
  [7:2:0.6] 1944 | Planning and Development Department | Ardeshir Dalal; abolished 1946
  [7:2:0.7] 1945 | People's Plan | M.N. Roy (Indian Federation of Labour) – Marxist; priority to agriculture
  [7:2:0.8] 1946 | Advisory Planning Board | K.C. Neogy – recommended the Planning Commission
  [7:2:0.9] 1947 | Economic Programme Committee | Jawaharlal Nehru
  [7:2:0.10] 1950 | Sarvodaya Plan | Jayaprakash Narayan (inspired by Gandhi and Vinoba)
[7:2:1] - **First Five-Year Plan in the world: USSR (1928, Stalin)**; India from 1951.
[7:2:2] - Indian planning drew on **Soviet planning** and the **Fabian socialism of Sidney and Beatrice Webb** (Nehru) – not Lenin's NEP.
[7:2:3] - **Mahatma Gandhi opposed centralised planned development** – self-reliance meant simple living, local resources and local production.
[7:2:4] - Supporters of planning: industrialists (Bombay Plan) and socialists; Gandhian and Sarvodaya plans = **decentralised alternative**.
[7:2:5] > Bose initiated, Nehru chaired the NPC; M.N. Roy = People's Plan, not a Bombay Plan signatory.
[7:2:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.1-4; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.28 (Visvesvaraya first attempt; NPC 1938 Nehru); app notes 3:7 sub 0, 3:8 sub 0
## SUB 3 | Which Body Did What – PC, NDC, Finance Commission, Finance Ministry
[7:3:0] TABLE head: Task | Body
  [7:3:0.0] Assess resources; prepare the draft plan | Planning Commission
  [7:3:0.1] Guidelines incl. assessment of resources; final approval of the plan | NDC
  [7:3:0.2] Plan becomes operative | Union Cabinet, then Parliament
  [7:3:0.3] Share of States in taxes; principles of distribution of net tax proceeds; grants | Finance Commission (Article 280, constitutional)
  [7:3:0.4] Suggestions on transfer and allocation of financial resources to Union and States | Finance Commission
  [7:3:0.5] Control of government expenditure; budgetary allocation | Finance Ministry
  [7:3:0.6] Allocation of central funds to States now (after the Planning Commission) | Union Finance Ministry (devolution on Finance Commission advice)
[7:3:1] - **Flow:** Commission drafts → **NDC approves** → Cabinet → Parliament. So the bodies "associated with planning" = **NDC and Parliament** (UPSC 2014), not the Finance Commission or the line ministries.
[7:3:2] TABLE head: Basis | Planning Commission | Finance Commission
  [7:3:2.0] Nature | Non-constitutional, permanent | Constitutional (Art. 280), periodic
  [7:3:2.1] Dealt with | Plan resources, planning | Tax devolution, grants (old non-Plan)
[7:3:3] - Finance Commission decides the **method and formula** for sharing tax proceeds between Centre and States.
[7:3:4] - **State plans:** State Planning Board (**CM as Chairman**, Vice-Chairman, 6-9 members) drafts; the **Chief Minister** sends the State draft plan to the Planning Commission.
[7:3:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.4; Rocket Indian Economy #23 p.33 (PC vs FC); NextGen Polity - APPSC 20; app notes 3:7 sub 3, sub 6
## SUB 4 | Deputy Chairmen and Members
[7:4:0] TABLE head: Deputy Chairman | Period / note
  [7:4:0.0] Gulzarilal Nanda | First (1950)
  [7:4:0.1] V.T. Krishnamachari | 1953-60 – Second Plan period
  [7:4:0.2] D.T. Lakdawala | 1977-80 – Rolling Plan (Janata)
  [7:4:0.3] Manmohan Singh | Jan 1985 – Aug 1987
  [7:4:0.4] Pranab Mukherjee | June 1991 – May 1996
  [7:4:0.5] Montek Singh Ahluwalia | July 2004 – May 2014 – last
[7:4:1] - **P. Chidambaram** was never Deputy Chairman; **A.M. Khusro, N.K.P. Salve, M.S. Ahluwalia** were never **Chairman** (only the PM is).
[7:4:2] - Deputy Chairman = de facto head, **Cabinet-minister rank**, attended Cabinet without a vote.
[7:4:3] - Composition: PM, Deputy Chairman, Finance and Planning Ministers ex officio, expert members, Member-Secretary; **States / Governors not represented**.
[7:4:4] - Last Plan: **Twelfth (2012-17)**.
[7:4:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.28 (first and last Deputy Chairman); Planning Commission list of Deputy Chairmen (planningcommission.nic.in, archived); app notes 3:8 sub 1
## SUB 5 | Planning, the Constitution and Decentralised Planning
[7:5:0] - "**Economic and social planning**" = **Concurrent List (List III), Entry 20**, Seventh Schedule.
[7:5:1] - **Article 39** (DPSP) – source of plan objectives; **Article 40** – village panchayats, the village as the primary unit.
[7:5:2] - **Not mentioned in the Constitution:** **NDC, Planning Commission, Zonal Councils** (all three; Zonal Councils are **statutory** – States Reorganisation Act 1956); NITI Governing Council is not statutory.
[7:5:3] - **Bottom-up planning** is a goal not yet achieved; the village is accepted as a suitable unit (UPPSC 1998 key: A and R true, R explains A).
[7:5:4] TABLE head: Body | Article | Rule
  [7:5:4.0] District Planning Committee | 243ZD | Four-fifths elected from elected members of district panchayat and municipalities; chairperson sends the draft district plan to the State
  [7:5:4.1] Metropolitan Planning Committee | 243ZE | Metro areas of 10 lakh+; two-thirds elected
[7:5:5] - **74th Amendment (1992)** inserted **Part IXA** (municipalities), which holds the DPC and MPC.
[7:5:6] - **Balwantrai Mehta (1957):** planning to PRIs (accepted Jan 1958); **JP Narayan Committee (1961):** flagged centralisation; **Thungon (1988):** **Zila Parishad** the pivot of planning.
[7:5:7] - **SFDA, DPAP, ITDP, IADP** ran **outside the panchayats**; **73rd and 74th Amendments** restored local bodies' role.
[7:5:8] - Levels: Centre (Planning Commission → NITI Aayog) → State Planning Boards → DPC / MPC → Mandal plans, **GPDP**.
[7:5:9] - **AP:** State Planning Commission in the **Fifth Plan**; first district-level planning in the **Sixth Plan – Rs 1 crore to each of 23 districts**; Economic Regions – **Visakhapatnam 10 districts** (NITI-assisted, launched 13 Nov 2025), **Amaravati 9**, Tirupati 10.
[7:5:10] - **Kerala People's Plan Campaign:** 17 Aug 1996; 35-40% of plan outlay to local bodies.
[7:5:11] - **NITI State Support Mission** (2026) helps States set up **State Institutions for Transformation**.
[7:5:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.4 (JP Committee, 73rd/74th); Constitution of India (List III Entry 20; Arts 40, 243ZD, 243ZE); app notes 3:7 sub 4, 6, 7, 3:8 sub 9
## SUB 6 | Criticism and the Shift to NITI Aayog
[7:6:0] - Epithets: "**super cabinet**", "economic cabinet", "parallel cabinet", "**fifth wheel of the coach**" – for **centralising decisions** and eroding **State autonomy**.
[7:6:1] - **Nehru (1958)** admitted the Commission had become a kind of "super-cabinet"; the Advisory Planning Board's "**common plan**" idea bred lasting Centre-State tension.
[7:6:2] - **NITI Aayog:** think tank; **bottom-up**, **cooperative (and competitive) federalism**; **no financial powers** – not a fund allocator.
[7:6:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.5; Rocket Indian Economy #24 p.34; app notes 3:7 sub 5

=== NOTES 3:8 | Objectives of Indian Planning; Types of Planning (imperative, Indicative, Perspective, Rolling)
## SUB 0 | Rolling Plan (1978-80)
[8:0:0] TABLE head: Particular | Fact
  [8:0:0.0] Suggested by | Gunnar Myrdal, for backward countries – "Indian Economic Planning in its Broader Setting"
  [8:0:0.1] Introduced by | Janata Party government (ended the Fifth Plan a year early)
  [8:0:0.2] Deputy Chairman then | D.T. Lakdawala – credited with introducing it in India
  [8:0:0.3] Period | April 1978 – March 1980 (1978-80)
  [8:0:0.4] Name | Janata Sixth Plan (1978-83) = "the rolling plan" – the novel idea of the draft Sixth Plan
  [8:0:0.5] Outlay | Rs 12,177 crore; stressed employment
  [8:0:0.6] End | Janata fall → 1979-80 annual plan → Congress Sixth Plan from 1 April 1980
[8:0:1] # Three plans in one (all four book statements are correct)
[8:0:2] - 1. A plan for the **current year** (with the annual budget).
[8:0:3] - 2. A plan for a fixed number of years (**3, 4 or 5**), **revised every year** as the economy requires and extended by one year.
[8:0:4] - 3. A **perspective plan** of **10, 15 or 20 years**.
[8:0:5] - Rolling plan **changes both its allocations and its targets every year** → flexibility; criticism: yearly revision **weakened commitment** to targets.
[8:0:6] > Myrdal suggested – Janata introduced – Lakdawala Deputy Chairman – 1978-80. Not Indira, Rajiv or the National Front; Madhu Dandavate was Deputy Chairman only in 1990-91.
[8:0:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.16 (Rolling Plan 1978-80); NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.38 (Myrdal; April 1978 – April 1980); app notes 3:8 sub 8
## SUB 1 | Objectives of Planning
[8:1:0] - **Four long-term objectives:** **growth, modernisation, self-reliance, social justice (equity)** – plus employment and removal of poverty; the long-term objective = **all** of high standard of living, social justice and self-reliance.
[8:1:1] - **Economic growth = principal objective**; the others are supporting.
[8:1:2] - Chief objectives also listed in the Plans: **reduce inequalities of income and wealth**, higher growth, **removal of poverty**, employment. "**To achieve social equality**" is not a stated economic objective of the Plans.
[8:1:3] - Major objective of the Five Year Plans (book key): **self-reliance** and less dependence on foreign sources.
[8:1:4] - Declared aim (1950 resolution): rapid rise in the **standard of living** through efficient use of resources, more production and **employment for all**.
[8:1:5] - **Planning** = a conscious effort of the State with a **set of goals**, a **set of means** and a **specific time period**.
[8:1:6] - Initial strategy of the 1950s: **rapid industrialisation** (Second Plan); cornerstone = **central economic planning**.
[8:1:7] # Socialistic pattern of society
[8:1:8] - Adopted at the **Avadi Congress (1955)** → **Second Plan (1956-61)** and **IPR 1956**.
[8:1:9] - Core element: **social gain, not private profit**, as the test of development (not communes or nationalising all firms).
[8:1:10] - Planning for socialism aims at a **national minimum standard of living** for all (NPC 1938, Bombay Plan).
[8:1:11] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.4-6; NCERT Class 11 Indian Economic Development ch.2 (goals of Five Year Plans); Second Five Year Plan ch.2 (socialist pattern – social gain, reduction of inequalities); app notes 3:8 sub 2
## SUB 2 | Perspective Planning and Plan Horizons
[8:2:0] TABLE head: Plan | Horizon
  [8:2:0.0] Annual plan | Short term – one year
  [8:2:0.1] Five Year Plan | Medium term
  [8:2:0.2] Perspective plan | Long term – 15 to 20 years or more; five-year and annual plans carved out of it
[8:2:1] - Perspective planning was a **Planning Commission function**.
[8:2:2] - **Long-term perspective plan 1985-2000** accompanied the **Seventh Plan**.
[8:2:3] - **AP now:** **Swarna Andhra Vision 2047** – GSDP **Rs 308.11 lakh crore by 2047-48**; ten "**Padi Sutralu**"; targets (10 Feb 2026): **15%** growth, GSDP **Rs 31 lakh crore**, PCI Rs 5.15 lakh by 2030.
[8:2:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.5, p.20 (15-year perspective 1985-2000); app notes 3:8 sub 7
[8:2:5] - **Plan horizon** = the **time frame** of a plan (5 years for a Five Year Plan, 15-20 years for a perspective plan) – not its size, outlay or growth target.
## SUB 3 | Plans – Thrust, Models and Financing
[8:3:0] TABLE head: Plan | Main thrust / model
  [8:3:0.0] First (from 1 April 1951) | Agriculture, irrigation, power; Harrod-Domar (growth = savings rate ÷ capital-output ratio); 3.6% achieved vs 2.1% target
  [8:3:0.1] Second (1956-61) | Rapid industrialisation, socialistic pattern – Feldman-Mahalanobis (heavy industry, public sector, import substitution; not export promotion); forex crisis cuts 1958
  [8:3:0.2] Third (1961-66) | Self-reliance, food self-sufficiency
  [8:3:0.3] Fourth (1969-74) | Target-group approach (SFDA, MFAL, DPAP); reduce dependence on foreign aid
  [8:3:0.4] Fifth (1974-79) | Self-reliance + raising consumption of the poor; direct attack on poverty; Tribal Sub-Plan
  [8:3:0.5] Sixth (1980-85) | Poverty alleviation; Minimum Needs Programme, IRDP
  [8:3:0.6] Ninth (1997-2002) | Seven Basic Minimum Services
  [8:3:0.7] Twelfth (2012-17) | Faster, sustainable, more inclusive growth – last
[8:3:1] - **Trickle-down** (First–Third Plans; Community Development Programme 1952) → target-group (Fourth) → direct attack (Fifth, Sixth).
[8:3:2] - **Wage-goods model:** **C.N. Vakil and P.R. Brahmananda** – alternative to Mahalanobis.
[8:3:3] - **Plan financing:** domestic budgetary resources, PSU surpluses, **market borrowing**, **deficit financing (borrowing from the RBI = money creation)**, foreign aid.
[8:3:4] - **Eighth Plan (1992-97):** largest source of public sector outlay = **government borrowings** (Rs 2,02,255 crore of Rs 4,34,100 crore, **46.6%**); deficit financing only Rs 20,000 crore.
[8:3:5] - **PURA** (Providing Urban Amenities in Rural Areas, 2003): idea of **A.P.J. Abdul Kalam**, announced by PM Vajpayee.
[8:3:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.6-24; Eighth Five Year Plan Vol.1 ch.4 (financing of public sector outlay, 1991-92 prices) via Economic Survey 2000-01 Appendix 2.11; app notes 3:8 sub 3, 4, 10
## SUB 4 | Imperative vs Indicative Planning
[8:4:0] TABLE head: Feature | Imperative (directive / target) | Indicative
  [8:4:0.0] Market mechanism | Entirely replaced by a command hierarchy | Plan improves the market
  [8:4:0.1] Ownership | State / collective | Mixed
  [8:4:0.2] Targets | Binding quotas, allocation, prices | Guidelines and signals
  [8:4:0.3] Examples | USSR (1928, Stalin), China | Mixed economies; France
  [8:4:0.4] India | Public sector in early Plans | From the Eighth Plan (1992-97) after 1991
[8:4:1] - A **Five-Year Plan** is an **indicative plan of action** reflecting the government's intent.
[8:4:2] - Soviet collectivisation (Stalin): collective farms = **Kolkhoz** (state farms = sovkhoz).
[8:4:3] - **Regional planning** first in the USA (1916+; Tennessee Valley).
[8:4:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.28, p.58 (imperative vs indicative); NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.6; Britannica "kolkhoz"; app notes 3:8 sub 5
## SUB 5 | Other Types of Planning
[8:5:0] TABLE head: Pair | Distinction
  [8:5:0.0] Centralised vs decentralised | One central authority, top-down vs decisions shared with lower governments / private units within a broad policy
  [8:5:0.1] Financial vs physical | Allocation in money terms vs in real terms (materials, manpower, output)
  [8:5:0.2] Direction vs inducement | Command (imperative) vs incentives guiding private units (indicative)
  [8:5:0.3] Democratic vs totalitarian | Through elected bodies and consent (India) vs state command
[8:5:1] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.58; app notes 3:8 sub 6
[8:5:2] - Indian planning is an **open process** – drafts are published and debated by the public, the NDC and Parliament.

  The Second Five Year Plan was based on which growth model?
  Harrod-Domar model | *Feldman-Mahalanobis model | Wage goods model | Lewis model
  x: The Second Plan used the Feldman-Mahalanobis model; Harrod-Domar was the First Plan's.
[f76429eb3910b] sub3
  Who put forward the wage goods model as an alternative to the Mahalanobis strategy?
  *C.N. Vakil and P.R. Brahmananda | Feldman and Mahalanobis | Harrod and Domar | K.N. Raj and Amartya Sen
  x: C.N. Vakil and P.R. Brahmananda proposed expanding wage goods first.
[ff9fd1d41938f] sub3
  Which of the following was NOT given priority under the Mahalanobis strategy?
  Heavy and capital-goods industries | A large public sector | Import substitution | *Export promotion
  x: Mahalanobis stressed heavy industry, the public sector and import substitution; export promotion was not a priority.
[fe7ee18b2cebb] sub3
  In which year did a foreign-exchange crisis force cuts in the Mahalanobis-based Second Plan?
  1956 | *1958 | 1961 | 1966
  x: The foreign-exchange crisis forced cuts in 1958; the model was replaced by the Third Plan (1961).
[n48b074df22d] sub3
  In the financing of India's Five Year Plans, what did 'deficit financing' mean?
  *Borrowing from the RBI, that is, money creation | Market borrowing | Public sector surpluses | Foreign aid
  x: Deficit financing was borrowing from the RBI; foreign aid and domestic savings (including market borrowing and PSU surpluses) were the other sources.
[f46070576b293] sub3
  Which Plan aimed to reduce India's dependence on foreign aid?
  First | *Fourth | Second | Eighth
  x: The Fourth Plan aimed to reduce dependence on foreign aid.
[g8d59f2424d5a] sub3
  What was the largest source of public sector outlay in the Eighth Five Year Plan?
  *Market borrowing | Deficit financing | Foreign aid | Public sector surpluses
  x: In the Eighth Plan, government (market) borrowings were the largest source of public sector outlay (Rs 2,02,255 crore, 46.6%); deficit financing was only Rs 20,000 crore.
[n8c271f451ad] sub4
  What is planning that works through binding quotas and allocation called?
  Indicative planning | Rolling planning | Perspective planning | *Imperative planning
  x: Imperative planning uses binding quotas; indicative planning only guides the private sector.
[n37c1d848089] sub4
  Which of the following pairs is correctly matched with respect to the type of planning?
  Imperative planning — mixed ownership and non-binding signals | Indicative planning — state or collective ownership only | *Imperative planning — USSR under Stalin and China | Indicative planning — complete command hierarchy
  x: The USSR under Stalin, beginning with the 1928 planning experience, and China are examples associated with imperative planning. Indicative planning is generally associated with mixed economies and market guidance.
[n86dab24953f] sub4
  From which Plan did India shift from imperative features towards indicative planning?
  First Plan after the adoption of the Harrod-Domar model | *Eighth Plan after the 1991 reforms | Fourth Plan after the Green Revolution | Twelfth Plan after the abolition of the Planning Commission
  x: India shifted to indicative planning from the Eighth Plan, covering 1992–97, following the economic reforms of 1991.
[fab90a26de60b] sub4
  What happens to the market mechanism in imperative planning?
  *Entirely replaced by command | Improved by the plan | Guided by incentives | Left fully free
  x: Imperative planning replaces the market by command; indicative planning improves it.
[f6c2e4353d72c] sub4
  Which country first adopted imperative planning in 1928?
  China | France | *USSR | Japan
  x: The USSR under Stalin began imperative planning in 1928.
[n83ea8f36d1d] sub5
  What is the allocation of resources in real terms (materials, manpower and output targets) called?
  *Physical planning | Planning by inducement | Financial planning | Democratic planning
  x: Physical planning allocates in real terms; allocation in money terms is financial planning.
[n4fa7696e2cc] sub5
  What does decentralised planning mean?
  All decisions are taken by one central authority | *Decisions are distributed to lower governments or private units within a broad policy | The plan is revised every year and extended by one year | The plan covers a period of 15–20 years
  x: Decentralised planning distributes decision-making to lower governments or private units within a broad policy framework; centralised planning concentrates it in one authority.
[f9b4c62cc85d5] sub5
  Planning in which incentives guide private units, instead of command, is called planning by:
  *Inducement | Direction | Physical allocation | Centralisation
  x: Planning by inducement uses incentives (indicative); planning by direction uses command (imperative).
[fef4c9dd0ce0c] sub5
  What is the allocation of resources in money terms called?
  Physical planning | Imperative planning | Perspective planning | *Financial planning
  x: Financial planning allocates in money terms; physical planning in real terms.
[g126182e74a46] sub5
  What type of planning is it when plans are made through elected bodies and with the consent of the people, as in India?
  *Democratic planning | Physical planning | Totalitarian planning | Imperative planning
  x: India follows democratic planning, where plans pass through elected bodies and consent, unlike totalitarian state command.

=== NOTES 3:9 | Plan Models – Harrod-domar, Mahalanobis, Wage-goods (vakil-brahmananda), Gandhian and Bombay Plans
## SUB 0 | Plan → Model It Was Based On
[9:0:0] TABLE head: Plan | Model / strategy
  [9:0:0.0] First (1951-56) | Harrod-Domar model (with modifications); priority agriculture
  [9:0:0.1] Second (1956-61) | P.C. Mahalanobis model (Nehru-Mahalanobis-Feldman; a modified Soviet / Russian model)
  [9:0:0.2] Third (1961-66), "Gadgil Yojana" | Sandee-Chakravarty model (NextGen); several books say "no specific model"; import substitution
  [9:0:0.3] Fourth (1969-74) | Alan S. Manne – Ashok Rudra (open) consistency model; Gadgil strategy (growth with stability)
  [9:0:0.4] Fifth (1974-79) | Dhar model
  [9:0:0.5] Eighth (1992-97) | John W. Miller model
[9:0:1] - **Order 1-2-3-4-5:** Harrod-Domar – Mahalanobis – Sandee-Chakravarty – Manne-Rudra – Dhar.
[9:0:2] - **Mahalanobis model launched in 1956** (Second Plan) laid the foundation of India's industrial modernisation; Mahalanobis = **architect of the Second Plan**.
[9:0:3] - Second Plan distractors: **wage-goods, Gadgil, CELP, Tendulkar, Kaldor, Joan Robinson, Solow** – the key is **Mahalanobis**. First Plan distractors: Lewis-Fei, Keynesian, Manne-Rudra, Gandhian, Sarvodaya – the key is **Harrod-Domar**.
[9:0:4] - **Solow (Solow-Swan):** neoclassical model on a **Cobb-Douglas** production function; **no Indian plan was based on it.**
[9:0:5] TABLE head: Model | Bottleneck it assumed | Priority
  [9:0:5.0] Harrod-Domar (Roy Harrod, Evsey Domar) | Limited savings | Raise savings and investment
  [9:0:5.1] Feldman-Mahalanobis | Capital goods | Heavy industry (steel, machinery)
  [9:0:5.2] Wage-goods (Vakil-Brahmananda, 1956) | Food / wage goods | Agriculture, employment
[9:0:6] > Match question (APPSC): Mahalanobis → Second; open consistency (Manne-Rudra) → Fourth; "no specific model" → Third; Harrod-Domar → First.
[9:0:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.28 (plan models); NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.6-10; NextGen Rocket Sheets Indian Economy #1 p.2; app notes 3:9 sub 0-1
## SUB 1 | Pre-independence Plans – Author and Year
[9:1:0] TABLE head: Plan | Year | Author(s) | Core idea
  [9:1:0.0] Visvesvaraya Plan ("Planned Economy for India") | 1934 | M. Visvesvaraya – "Father of Indian Planning" | First blueprint; double national income in a decade via industrialisation
  [9:1:0.1] FICCI proposal | 1934 | FICCI | Need for national planning
  [9:1:0.2] National Planning Committee (Congress) | 1938 | Set up under Subhas Chandra Bose; chaired by Nehru | Final report 1949
  [9:1:0.3] Bombay Plan ("A Plan of Economic Development for India") | 1944 (January; Part 2 Dec 1944) | 8 industrialists: J.R.D. Tata, G.D. Birla, Purshottamdas Thakurdas, Kasturbhai Lalbhai, Ardeshir Dalal, Lala Shri Ram, A.D. Shroff, John Mathai | Big State role: public investment in infrastructure and heavy/basic industry; protection of infant industry; treble national income in 15 years
  [9:1:0.4] Gandhian Plan | 1944 | Shriman Narayan Agarwal (not Gandhi himself) | Agriculture, cottage and village industries, decentralised self-contained villages
  [9:1:0.5] People's Plan | 1945 | M.N. Roy, for the Post-War Reconstruction Committee of the Indian Federation of Labour | Marxist socialism; basic necessities; equal weight to agriculture and industry
  [9:1:0.6] Sarvodaya Plan | 1950 | Jayaprakash Narayan | Gandhian constructive work + Vinoba Bhave's Sarvodaya
[9:1:1] - **Wrong pairs asked:** Bombay Plan – **Rajaji** ✗; Bombay Plan – **Dhirubhai Ambani** ✗ (he was not an architect); People's Plan – Gandhi/Mahalanobis ✗.
[9:1:2] - **All correct (APPSC Group 1):** Bombay Plan – **John Mathai** ✓ (a signatory), People's Plan – M.N. Roy ✓, Gandhian Plan – **Shriman Narayan** ✓, Second Plan – Mahalanobis ✓.
[9:1:3] - **Chronology:** NPC (1938) → Bombay and Gandhian Plans (1944) → People's Plan (1945) → Sarvodaya (1950) → First Plan (1951).
[9:1:4] - Precursors: **Karachi Resolution (1931)**; **Planning and Development Department (1944)** under **Ardeshir Dalal**; **Advisory Planning Board (1946)** headed by **K.C. Neogi**.
[9:1:5] - People's Plan echoed in the 1990s slogan "**reforms with a human face**".
[9:1:6] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.2-4; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.44; NextGen Rocket Sheets Indian Economy #22 p.32; Wikipedia "Bombay Plan" (citing Kudaisya, Business History Review 2014) for the 8 signatories; app notes 3:9 sub 6
## SUB 2 | Mahalanobis Model
[9:2:0] # Strategy and sectors
[9:2:1] - **Heavy sector strategy** / **unbalanced growth**: priority to **heavy capital goods industries** (steel, machinery) – most public-sector investment went there.
[9:2:2] - Originally conceived by **G.A. Feldman** (USSR, **1928**); Mahalanobis (**1953**) developed it independently – so the **Feldman-Mahalanobis** model, based on the **Russian (Soviet)** model.
[9:2:3] - **Four sectors:** (1) capital goods, (2) factory consumer goods, (3) household / small-scale consumer goods, (4) services.
[9:2:4] - **Stalin's USSR** began five-year plans in **1928** – **imperative** planning; India follows **indicative** planning.
[9:2:5] TABLE head: Included / emphasised | Excluded / neglected
  [9:2:5.0] Industrialisation through heavy industries | Foreign trade – assumed a closed economy
  [9:2:5.1] Infrastructure, basic and key industries | Agriculture (not the priority)
  [9:2:5.2] Dominant public sector (socialistic pattern, IPR 1956), self-reliance, import substitution | Exports / export promotion
  [9:2:5.3] Investment from 7% to 11% of national income | Village and cottage industries were not top priority (left consumer goods to them)
[9:2:6] # Criticisms
[9:2:7] - **Neglect of agriculture and small-scale industries**, **over-emphasis on heavy industry**, **neglect of social objectives** (employment, equity) – food and capital shortages, inflation in the late 1950s.
[9:2:8] - The emphasis on **growth** itself was the plan's aim, **not** the criticism (APPSC G2 Mains 2017).
[9:2:9] # Second Plan outcome
[9:2:10] - Growth: target **4.5%**, achieved **4.27%** (most books; NextGen prints 4.21%).
[9:2:11] - Steel plants **Bhilai, Durgapur, Rourkela**; **Chittaranjan** – locomotives; **Perambur** – coaches; Atomic Energy Commission.
[9:2:12] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.8-9; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.46 (unbalanced growth); app notes 3:9 sub 3, 8
[9:2:13] - **Closed economy:** for nearly four decades after independence (till the 1991 reforms) India was a largely **closed**, inward-looking economy.
[9:2:14] - **Nehru-Mahalanobis strategy** also sought to turn **dependence on metropolitan capitalism** into **independent development** and to move from **semi-feudal agriculture to capitalist farming** – both are its components.
## SUB 3 | Harrod-Domar Model and Capital-Output Ratio
[9:3:0] - **g = s / k** (g = growth rate, s = **savings** rate, k = **capital-output ratio**): growth **rises with savings**, **falls with a higher COR** – so COR fixes growth **along with savings**.
[9:3:1] - **Capital-output ratio** = capital needed to raise output by **one unit** per annum.
[9:3:2] TABLE head: Basis | High COR | Low COR
  [9:3:2.0] Meaning | More capital per unit of output; investment gives less output | Less capital per unit of output
  [9:3:2.1] Capital productivity | Lower | Higher
  [9:3:2.2] Growth | Slower, even with high savings | Higher potential
[9:3:3] - ★ Low growth despite high savings and capital formation = **high capital-output ratio** (UPSC 1995, 2018).
[9:3:4] - ★ Slow per-capita income growth = **high COR + high population growth** (UPSC 1993: "1 and 2").
[9:3:5] # First Plan
[9:3:6] - Started **1 April 1951**; priority **agriculture**, irrigation and power; investment target 7% of national income.
[9:3:7] - Faced refugees, food shortage, inflation; succeeded mainly due to **good harvests in its last two years**.
[9:3:8] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.56 (high COR); NextGen Rocket Sheets Indian Economy p.71 (COR); NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.6-7; app notes 3:9 sub 2
[9:3:9] - **W.A. Lewis:** development means raising saving and investment from about **4-5% to 12-15%** of national income (Rostow's take-off needs investment above 10%).
## SUB 4 | P.C. Mahalanobis – ISI and Statistics Day
[9:4:0] TABLE head: Item | Fact
  [9:4:0.0] Role | Architect of the Second Plan; called the architect of Indian planning
  [9:4:0.1] Indian Statistical Institute | Founded 17 Dec 1931, Kolkata, by P.C. Mahalanobis; journal Sankhya; Institution of National Importance by the ISI Act, 1959
  [9:4:0.2] National Income Committee (1949) | He chaired it → recommended the National Sample Survey; first NSS round 1950-51
  [9:4:0.3] Honours | Padma Vibhushan (1968), FRS (1945)
  [9:4:0.4] National Statistics Day | 29 June (his birth anniversary), since 2007
[9:4:1] - Statistics Day themes: **2018 – "Quality Assurance in Official Statistics"**; 2026 – "**Unlocking the Potential of Administrative Data**".
[9:4:2] - **ISI Bill, 2026** introduced in Lok Sabha on **2 Aug 2026** to replace the 1959 Act; referred to the **Standing Committee on Finance** (1 Sep 2026).
[9:4:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 02 p.33; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 03 p.36; MoSPI Statistics Day 2018 release (gktoday summary); app notes 3:9 sub 4
## SUB 5 | Other Growth Theories
[9:5:0] TABLE head: Theory | Name to remember
  [9:5:0.0] Secular stagnation | Alvin Hansen (1938)
  [9:5:0.1] Differential savings propensity (workers vs capitalists) | Nicholas Kaldor (distribution theory of growth)
  [9:5:0.2] Neoclassical growth | Solow (Cobb-Douglas)
[9:5:1] - **Cobb-Douglas** Q = A L^α K^β: **α + β = 1 → constant** returns to scale (>1 increasing, <1 decreasing).
[9:5:2] ~~ Sources: standard macroeconomics (Hansen 1938 AEA address; Kaldor 1956 "Alternative Theories of Distribution"); app notes 3:9 sub 1
[9:5:3] - **Input-output analysis (Wassily Leontief):** rests on **commodity balances** – each sector's output = intermediate use + final demand; used for plan consistency.
## SUB 6 | Gandhian Economics and the Janata Strategy
[9:6:0] - Gandhian economy rests on **Trusteeship**: the wealthy act as trustees of wealth.
[9:6:1] - "**India's Economic Policy: The Gandhian Blue Print**" – **Charan Singh**; "**Gandhian Constitution for Free India**" – **Shriman Narayan Agarwal** (not M.N. Roy).
[9:6:2] - **Ram Rajya:** rule on moral authority and justice for the poorest; **not a Hindu state**.
[9:6:3] - **Janata alternative strategy (1977-80):** ended the Fifth Plan a year early (1978); **Rolling Plan 1978-80** (Gunnar **Myrdal's** concept); stressed **agriculture, rural development, employment, cottage and village industries, minimum needs** – **not** heavy industry, and **SEZs** (a 2000s idea) had no place in it.
[9:6:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.3; app notes 3:9 sub 6-7
## SUB 7 | Wage-goods Model
[9:7:0] - Authors: **C.N. Vakil and P.R. Brahmananda** – "**Planning for an Expanding Economy**" (**1956**).
[9:7:1] - Real constraint = supply of **wage goods** (food, essential consumer goods), **not capital goods**.
[9:7:2] - More wage goods → **disguised unemployed** put to productive work; stresses **employment**.
[9:7:3] - Priority: **agriculture and wage goods** vs Mahalanobis's heavy industry.
[9:7:4] ~~ Sources: app notes 3:9 sub 5

  What growth did the Second Five Year Plan achieve against its target?
  5.27 per cent against a target of 4.5 per cent | 4.5 per cent against a target of 4.27 per cent | 3.27 per cent against a target of 4.5 per cent | *4.27 per cent against a target of 4.5 per cent
  x: The Second Five Year Plan achieved 4.27 per cent growth against its target of 4.5 per cent.
[n8a86bcdf6e3] sub2
  Under the Second Five Year Plan, with the manufacture of what was Chittaranjan associated?
  Railway coaches | Steel | *Locomotives | Ships
  x: Chittaranjan made locomotives; Perambur made coaches and Bhilai, Durgapur and Rourkela were steel plants.
[ff74e54fffe68] sub2
  How many sectors did the Mahalanobis model have?
  Two | Three | *Four | Five
  x: Its four sectors were capital goods, factory consumer goods, household/small-scale consumer goods and services.
[fdba93b7da31f] sub2
  Consider the following statements about the Mahalanobis model:
1. Feldman (1928) and Mahalanobis (1953) developed it independently.
2. It left consumer goods to the small and cottage sector.
3. It gave top priority to agriculture.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  *1 and 2 only | 2 and 3 only | 1 and 3 only | 1, 2 and 3
  x: Statements 1 and 2 are correct; the model gave priority to the capital goods sector and neglected agriculture.
[fc92bc9cf8f5b] sub2
  Which of the following was NOT neglected by the Mahalanobis model?
  Agriculture | Exports | *Heavy industry | Consumer goods
  x: Heavy industry was its priority; agriculture, consumer goods and exports were neglected.
[g1e7623b5c2ea] sub2
  Which of the following steel plants was set up under the Second Five Year Plan?
  Bokaro | *Bhilai | Visakhapatnam | Salem
  x: Bhilai, Durgapur and Rourkela steel plants came up under the Mahalanobis-based Second Plan.
[g56277e5699c4] sub2
  What did the Perambur unit, set up under the Second Five Year Plan, manufacture?
  Fertilisers | *Railway coaches | Locomotives | Steel
  x: Under the Second Plan, Perambur made railway coaches and Chittaranjan made locomotives.
[n04aca12d2c0] sub2
  In which year did Stalin's USSR, whose system is described as imperative planning, begin five-year plans?
  1917 | 1924 | *1928 | 1936
  x: The USSR began five-year plans in 1928; India follows indicative planning.
[f2a6cfb460092] sub2
  Consider the following statements about types of planning:
1. India follows indicative planning.
2. Stalin's USSR followed indicative planning.
Which of the statements given above is/are correct?
  *1 only | 2 only | Both 1 and 2 | Neither 1 nor 2
  x: India follows indicative planning; Stalin's USSR, which began plans in 1928, followed imperative planning.
[n089d6e1b112] sub3
  In the Harrod-Domar model, other factors remaining constant, when does growth rise?
  the capital-output ratio rises | the savings rate falls | *the savings rate rises | savings are replaced by consumption
  x: Growth depends positively on the savings rate and negatively on the capital-output ratio.
[nindian-economy-68-2] sub3
  Which of the following statements regarding the Capital Output Ratio is incorrect?
  A high COR means that investment produces relatively less output. | A high COR indicates lower capital productivity. | A low COR indicates higher productivity of capital. | *A low COR means that more capital is needed for each unit of output.
  x: Option D is incorrect: a low COR means less capital is needed per unit of output, so capital is more productive. Needing more capital per unit means a high COR.
[nindian-economy-48-3] sub3
  What does a decrease in the Capital-Output Ratio mean per unit of output?
  More capital is needed | *Less capital is needed | The savings rate must rise | The same capital is needed
  x: A lower COR means less capital is needed per unit of output, which raises growth potential.
[fa83d1a8e99d9] sub3
  In the Harrod-Domar equation g = s / k, what does 'k' stand for?
  Savings rate | Rate of growth | *Capital-output ratio | Rate of investment
  x: In g = s / k, g is the growth rate, s the savings rate and k the capital-output ratio.
[f66a29b96612b] sub3
  What was the main reason for the success of the First Five Year Plan?
  *Good harvests in its last two years | Rapid growth of heavy industry | A large rise in exports | Nationalisation of banks
  x: Despite refugees, food shortage and inflation, the First Plan succeeded mainly due to good harvests in its last two years.
[nindian-economy-68-22] sub3
  What does a high Capital Output Ratio mean for the output produced by investment?
  relatively more output | *relatively less output | output independent of investment | faster growth from the same savings
  x: With a high COR each unit of investment yields relatively little output, so even high savings may give slow growth.
[n22f6b347302] sub4
  By which Act was the Indian Statistical Institute made an Institution of National Importance?
  *ISI Act, 1959 | NSS Act, 1950 | National Statistics Act, 2007 | Industrial Policy Resolution, 1956
  x: The Indian Statistical Institute was made an Institution of National Importance through the ISI Act of 1959.
[ne47e9361a8d] sub4
  When was the first round of the National Sample Survey, recommended by the National Income Committee, held?
  1949–50 | *1950–51 | 1951–52 | 1952–53
  x: The first NSS round was in 1950–51; Mahalanobis chaired the National Income Committee (1949).
[ndd61dbfc980] sub4

  M. Visvesvaraya | M.N. Roy | C.N. Vakil | *P.C. Mahalanobis
  x: National Statistics Day is observed on 29 June, the birth anniversary of P.C. Mahalanobis, since 2007.
[f2b32e2833774] sub4
  In which year was the Indian Statistical Institute founded in Kolkata?
  *1931 | 1949 | 1950 | 1959
  x: P.C. Mahalanobis founded the ISI on 17 December 1931; the ISI Act came in 1959.
[f4a28900aa756] sub4
  Since which year has National Statistics Day been observed in India?
  1959 | 1998 | *2007 | 2012
  x: National Statistics Day, 29 June, has been observed since 2007.
[f1a385cb40416] sub4
  P.C. Mahalanobis is known as the architect of which Five Year Plan?
  First | *Second | Third | Fourth
  x: Mahalanobis was the architect of the Second Plan (1956-61).
[g16bfb92a17ee] sub4
  Who chaired the National Income Committee of 1949, which recommended the National Sample Survey?
  *P.C. Mahalanobis | C.D. Deshmukh | V.K.R.V. Rao | D.R. Gadgil
  x: P.C. Mahalanobis chaired the National Income Committee (1949); it recommended the NSS, whose first round was held in 1950-51.

  x: National Statistics Day (29 June) 2026 had the theme 'Unlocking the Potential of Administrative Data'.

  x: Mahalanobis received the Padma Vibhushan in 1968; he became FRS in 1945.

  x: On 1 September 2026 the ISI Bill, 2026 was referred to the Standing Committee on Finance.
[n1750ce765a2] sub6
  In Gandhian thought, what does 'Ram Rajya' primarily denote?
  A state controlled by religious institutions | A theocratic Hindu state | A monarchy headed by a hereditary ruler | *Rule based on pure moral authority and justice for the poorest
  x: Ram Rajya refers to rule founded on moral authority and justice for the poorest; it does not mean the establishment of a Hindu state.
[n6b4f044c868] sub6
  Which of the following pairs is NOT correctly matched?
  Sarvodaya Plan — J.P. Narayan | Bombay Plan — Eight Bombay industrialists | People's Plan — M.N. Roy | *Gandhian Constitution for Free India — M.N. Roy

[fdd5958f3e4aa] sub6
  Who wrote 'Gandhian Constitution for Free India'?
  M.N. Roy | *Shriman Narayan Agarwal | Charan Singh | Jayaprakash Narayan
  x: Shriman Narayan Agarwal wrote it, not M.N. Roy; Charan Singh wrote 'The Gandhian Blue Print'.
[n90c36c2b371] sub6
  Which of the following was emphasised in the Janata Government's alternative strategy?
  Rapid expansion of heavy industry | Capital-goods industries | *Small and cottage industries | Large public-sector heavy plants
  x: The Janata strategy stressed agriculture, rural development, employment, small and cottage industries and minimum needs rather than heavy industry.
[nbb0ad049259] sub6
  For which period did the Janata Government, after ending the Fifth Plan a year early, adopt a Rolling Plan?
  1966–69 | *1978–80 | 1990–92 | 1974–78
  x: The Rolling Plan covered 1978–80, following a concept advocated by Gunnar Myrdal.
[fb2e2a0564656] sub6
  Whose concept was the Rolling Plan adopted by the Janata Government (1978-80)?
  *Gunnar Myrdal | P.C. Mahalanobis | D.P. Dhar | Ragnar Nurkse
  x: The Rolling Plan, adopted after the Fifth Plan was ended a year early, was Gunnar Myrdal's concept.
[n6ac95e177ba] sub7
  Who put forward the wage-goods model?
  Roy Harrod and Evsey Domar | P.C. Mahalanobis and M. Visvesvaraya | *C.N. Vakil and P.R. Brahmananda | J.R.D. Tata and G.D. Birla
  x: The wage-goods model was put forward by C.N. Vakil and P.R. Brahmananda in 1956.
[n6ece4204f43] sub7
  According to the wage-goods model, what is the main constraint on economic expansion?
  Capital goods | Foreign exchange | *Wage goods such as food and essential consumer goods | Skilled labour
  x: The wage-goods model treats the supply of food and essential consumer goods, not capital goods, as the real constraint on expansion.
[n1bd54e58483] sub7
  Which book is associated with the wage-goods model?
  The General Theory of Employment | *Planning for an Expanding Economy | A Plan of Economic Development for India | Planning and the Poor
  x: Vakil and Brahmananda put forward the wage-goods model in their 1956 work, Planning for an Expanding Economy.
[f81889e401dce] sub7
  In the wage-goods model, more wage goods would allow which group to be put to productive work?
  Industrial workers | Skilled labour | *Disguised unemployed | Public servants
  x: Vakil and Brahmananda argued that more wage goods would let the disguised unemployed be put to productive work.
[fa931fa62e615] sub7
  In contrast to Mahalanobis, the wage-goods model held that the real constraint was NOT the supply of:
  *Capital goods | Food | Essential consumer goods | Wage goods
  x: The wage-goods model saw food and essential consumer goods as the constraint, not capital goods.

=== NOTES 3:10 | Five Year Plans I to Xii – Objectives, Targets, Achievements and Failures of Each
## SUB 0 | Plans I to V and the Plan Holiday – Objectives and Firsts
[10:0:0] TABLE head: Plan | Period | Model | Main objective / tagline
  [10:0:0.0] First | 1951-56 (launched 1 April 1951) | Harrod-Domar | Agriculture and irrigation (with power, transport); food, refugee rehabilitation, price stability
  [10:0:0.1] Second | 1956-61 ("Mahalanobis Plan") | Nehru-Mahalanobis | Rapid industrialisation – heavy and basic industries; socialistic pattern of society
  [10:0:0.2] Third | 1961-66 ("Gadgil Yojana") | Sandy-Chakravarty | Self-reliant and self-generating economy ("make the economy independent"); self-sustaining growth; agriculture priority
  [10:0:0.3] Plan Holiday | 1 April 1966 – 31 March 1969 | – | Three Annual Plans; food problem, Green Revolution (1966-67); agriculture and industry equal weight
  [10:0:0.4] Fourth | 1969-74 (finally launched 1969) | Manne-Rudra (Gadgil strategy) | Twin objectives: growth with stability and progressive achievement of self-reliance; 14 banks nationalised 1969
  [10:0:0.5] Fifth | 1974-79 (ended 1978) | Dhar (final draft by D.P. Dhar) | Twin objectives: removal of poverty (Garibi Hatao) and self-reliance; control of inflation; zero net foreign aid
[10:0:1] # First Plan (1951-56)
[10:0:2] - **Draft outline** presented by the Planning Commission in **July 1951**; final plan December 1952.
[10:0:3] - Prepared to face **Partition (refugees)**, **inflation** and **food shortage**; priority **agriculture and irrigation** – about **one-third** of outlay (highest agriculture share of any plan); **Bhakra Nangal, Hirakud**.
[10:0:4] - **Land reform:** the First Plan first proposed **co-operative village management** (village land managed co-operatively) – not the Second Plan.
[10:0:5] - Succeeded mainly due to **good harvests** in its last two years.
[10:0:6] # Second Plan (1956-61)
[10:0:7] - **Mahalanobis** two- and four-sector model; **heavy industry-led development**; **maximum emphasis on the industrial sector**; the **first major industrial plan**; primary responsibility of development shifted to the **public sector** ("public sector plan").
[10:0:8] - From the Second Plan a **determined thrust towards substitution (import substitution) of basic and capital goods** industries.
[10:0:9] - Goal of **socialistic pattern of society** (with IPR 1956).
[10:0:10] - **Targets pruned** because of an **acute shortage of foreign exchange** (1957-58 crisis) and rising prices.
[10:0:11] # Third, Fourth and Fifth Plans
[10:0:12] - **Third:** self-reliant, self-generating economy – self-reliance / self-sustaining growth first made the objective; hit by **two wars (1962, 1965)** and the 1965-66 drought.
[10:0:13] - **Fourth:** "growth with stability and progressive achievement of self-reliance"; **correcting the earlier trend of increased concentration of wealth and economic power**; **exports to grow about 7 per cent a year** for balance on foreign account; **Growth Centre approach** for regional disparities; target-group programmes.
[10:0:14] - **Fifth:** **removal of poverty for the first time** the basic objective; "**Garibi Hatao**" – Indira Gandhi's slogan of the **1971 elections**; **anti-poverty programmes** and serious concern for **unemployment** start here; high priority to **bring inflation under control** and achieve stability.
[10:0:15] - **Financial sector** was **not** first made an integral part of the plan in the Fifth (false statement, UPSC 2019).
[10:0:16] > Twin objectives (APPSC): Fourth = growth with stability + progressive self-reliance; Fifth = poverty removal + self-reliance. Garibi Hatao = Fifth, not Fourth or Sixth.
[10:0:17] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.6-16; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.45, p.51-52, p.60; NextGen Rocket Sheets Indian Economy #1 p.2, #22 p.32; UPSC CSE 2019 key; First Plan ch.12 (co-operative village management); app notes 3:10 sub 0, 3
[10:0:18] - **Second Plan drafting:** led by **P.C. Mahalanobis** (First Plan: **K.N. Raj**); heavy industry in the public sector; balancing industry and agriculture proved difficult.
[10:0:19] - **Second Plan:** **district and village plans** first prepared in its preparatory exercise.
[10:0:20] - **Third Plan:** first stated that **foodgrain producers must get a reasonable (remunerative) return** – the root of the price-support policy (APPSC key).
## SUB 1 | Plans VI to XII – Objectives and Firsts
[10:1:0] TABLE head: Plan | Period | Main objective / tagline
  [10:1:0.0] Sixth | 1980-85 | Poverty eradication and technological self-reliance; power significant
  [10:1:0.1] Seventh | 1985-90 | "Food, Work and Productivity"; employment thrust
  [10:1:0.2] Annual Plans | 1990-92 | Employment and social transformation
  [10:1:0.3] Eighth | 1992-97 | Human resource development; human development at the core; John W. Miller model; indicative planning
  [10:1:0.4] Ninth | 1997-2002 | "Growth with Social Justice and Equity" (United Front Government); final NDC approval 19 Feb 1999
  [10:1:0.5] Tenth | 2002-07 | 8% GDP growth; double per capita income in 10 years; first monitorable targets
  [10:1:0.6] Eleventh | 2007-12 | "Towards Faster and More Inclusive Growth"
  [10:1:0.7] Twelfth | 2012-17 | "Faster, Sustainable and More Inclusive Growth"
[10:1:1] # Sixth to Ninth
[10:1:2] - **Sixth:** first plan to focus on **gender issues** and inter/intra-State inequalities.
[10:1:3] - **Eighth:** shift from heavy industry to **infrastructure** (UPSC 2010); **generation of productive employment** = central element of its strategy; growth "on two legs" with a safety net.
[10:1:4] - **Ninth:** agriculture growth **decelerated**; seven Basic Minimum Services; Women's Component Plan.
[10:1:5] # Tenth
[10:1:6] - **Monitorable targets** for 11 indicators, set for the Centre **and for each State** in consultation with the States (state-wise targets – unique feature).
[10:1:7] - Agriculture the prime moving force; Governance as a factor of development.
[10:1:8] # Eleventh and Twelfth
[10:1:9] - **Eleventh:** top priority to **agriculture** (NDC Approach Paper); social sector first, education on top.
[10:1:10] - **Twelfth:** growth strategy depends crucially on **productivity gains**; three scenarios – "Strong Inclusive Growth", "Insufficient Action", "**Policy Logjam**" (Scenario 3); households with electricity **56% (2001) → 67% (2011)**.
[10:1:11] - **Twelfth targets:** Approach Paper **9%** GDP → NDC (Dec 2012) **8%**; agriculture **4%**; **manufacturing 10%**; **50 million** non-farm jobs; core aims – health, education, skills, PPP in infrastructure, fiscal correction (a "gene revolution" is **not** among them).
[10:1:12] > Eleventh = faster and **more inclusive**; Twelfth adds **sustainable**; Ninth = growth with social justice; Eighth = human development.
[10:1:13] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.17-23; NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 02 p.1-12; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.51, p.56; Twelfth Plan Vol. I (scenarios, productivity); app notes 3:10 sub 1, 3, 7
[10:1:14] - **Ninth:** decentralisation of planning and implementation through PRIs; decentralised planning included under Special Area Development programmes.
[10:1:15] - **Infrastructure** = the thrust sector for overall development in the later Plans – Eighth onwards; the Twelfth Plan sought about **USD 1 trillion** of infrastructure investment, nearly half private.
[10:1:16] - **Sixth Plan / Budget 1983-84:** **energy** had the highest plan outlay (~28%).
[10:1:17] - **Twelfth Plan capabilities:** human, institutional and **infrastructure – 'a general capability enhancer for all agents'**.
[10:1:18] - **Twelfth Plan vision (25 monitorable targets):** real GDP **8%**, agriculture **4%**, manufacturing 10%, head-count poverty down **10 percentage points**.
[10:1:19] - **Tenth Plan:** first plan with a **full chapter on disaster management**.
[10:1:20] - **Tenth Plan employment target:** **gainful and high-quality employment** at least equal to the addition to the labour force (first time).
[10:1:21] - **Agriculture's share of GDP (2001):** about **25%** (Tenth Plan Approach Paper).
[10:1:22] - **Twelfth Plan health:** **Universal Health Coverage** a main aim (HLEG, K. Srinath Reddy).
[10:1:23] - **Twelfth Plan poverty target:** head-count ratio of consumption poverty down **10 percentage points** by 2017.
[10:1:24] - **Eleventh Plan employment strategy:** shift **surplus labour** from agriculture to non-agriculture through **skill development**.
[10:1:25] - **Tenth Plan financing:** investment rate projected at **28.41%** of GDP against domestic savings of **26.84%** → investment **higher** than savings; the gap met by foreign savings.
[10:1:26] - **Sixth Plan:** a **direct attack on poverty** through an expanding economy (IRDP, NREP).
[10:1:27] - **Tenth Plan Approach Paper:** agriculture about **one-fourth (25%)** of GDP.
[10:1:28] - **Sixth Plan targets:** GDP growth **5.2%**; exports to grow about **10% a year** (APPSC G1 key).
## SUB 2 | Growth: Target vs Achieved, Outlays
[10:2:0] TABLE head: Plan | Target (%) | Achieved (%)
  [10:2:0.0] I | 2.1 | 3.6
  [10:2:0.1] II | 4.5 | 4.27 (NextGen 4.21)
  [10:2:0.2] III | 5.6 | 2.2 (lowest; biggest shortfall)
  [10:2:0.3] Plan Holiday | – | 3.69
  [10:2:0.4] IV | 5.7 | 3.3 (several books 2.05)
  [10:2:0.5] V | 4.4 | 4.8
  [10:2:0.6] VI | 5.2 | 5.7
  [10:2:0.7] VII | 5.0 | 6.0
  [10:2:0.8] VIII | 5.6 | 6.8 (6.68 ≈ 6.7)
  [10:2:0.9] IX | 6.5 | 5.4 (5.35)
  [10:2:0.10] X | 8.0 (cut to 7 at mid-term) | 7.6 (NextGen 7.7)
  [10:2:0.11] XI | 9.0 | 8.0 (7.9) – highest
  [10:2:0.12] XII | 9 (Approach Paper) → 8 (NDC, Dec 2012) | about 6.7
[10:2:1] - **Plans that beat targets:** **I, V, VI, VII, VIII**.
[10:2:2] - **Eighth Plan:** growth **6.7-6.8%** against 5.6% – a higher-than-expected rate and the plan-period peak; annual growth averaged ~7.5% in its last three years (1994-97); agriculture 3.6%, industry 8.5%; per capita output 3.9%.
[10:2:3] - **Ninth Plan:** services grew about **7.8%** a year; share of the public sector in GDCF fell from 44.6% (Sixth) to about **27.3%**.
[10:2:4] - **Eleventh Plan:** agriculture **3.3%** (Approach Paper / mid-term estimate; final **3.6-3.7%**) against 4% target.
[10:2:5] - **Fifth Plan outlay:** **industry and minerals ~25%** (Rs 10,201 cr of Rs 39,303 cr revised), power ~19%, agriculture + irrigation ~21%.
[10:2:6] - **Eighth Plan State Plans:** States' own contribution at 1993-94 prices **(-) 4%** (negative balance from current revenues).
[10:2:7] - **Highest GDP and per-capita growth:** **Eleventh Plan** (per capita GDP ~**7.6%** a year); Twelfth: GDP ~**6.7%**, per capita NNI ~**5.3%**.
[10:2:8] - **1950-51:** ~3.6%; lowest year since 1961 (excluding Covid) **1979, −5.2%**; 2020-21 −5.8%; 1990-91 1.1%; 2002-03 ~4%, 2003-04 **8.2%**.
[10:2:9] - **Highest average period:** **2004-05 to 2012-13**; plan-era average (I–XI) **~4.5%** vs ~1% before Independence.
[10:2:10] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.6-23; NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 02 p.2-10; Economic Survey 1997-98 (Eighth Plan); Economic Survey 2012-13 ch.8 (agriculture 3.6%); Fifth Plan ch.5 (agritech.tnau.ac.in); app notes 3:10 sub 5
[10:2:11] - **Ninth Plan ICOR:** about **4.0** in the Approach Paper (7% growth, 28.2% investment); final plan 4.3 with 6.5% growth.
[10:2:12] - **First Plan:** national income rose about **18%** over 1951-56 (about 3.6% a year).
[10:2:13] - **Tenth Plan sector targets:** **communication ~15%** (highest), financial services 11.7%, manufacturing 9.8%, trade 9.4%, agriculture ~4%.
[10:2:14] - **Eleventh Plan agriculture** (3.3% vs 2.4% in the Tenth): better performance of **crops and livestock** (NFSM, RKVY).
[10:2:15] - **Inflation (WPI average):** Ninth Plan about **4-5%**, Tenth Plan about **5%**.
[10:2:16] - **Eighth Plan targets:** GDP **5.6%**, industry **7.5%** a year.
[10:2:17] - **Eleventh Plan industry:** target about **10%** a year; manufacturing averaged ~**7.7%** (to 2009-10).
[10:2:18] - **Agriculture negative growth:** **Third Plan** (1961-66), about (-)1% a year after the 1965-66 drought.
[10:2:19] - **2000-02:** GDP grew only about 4-5% a year – roughly half the Tenth Plan's 8% target.
[10:2:20] - **Eighth Plan public-sector outlay:** final **Rs 4,34,100 crore** (1991-92 prices); the figure keyed in older exams, about **Rs 3,45,000 crore**, is the earlier proposal.
[10:2:21] - **Plan outlay** = **public-sector** allocation – to PSUs, agriculture, irrigation, infrastructure and the weaker sections; the plans did **not allocate resources to the private corporate sector** (it got only targets and licences).
## SUB 3 | Programme or Policy → Plan That Introduced It
[10:3:0] TABLE head: Programme / component | Year | Plan
  [10:3:0.0] Community Development Programme | 2 Oct 1952 | First
  [10:3:0.1] New agricultural strategy (HYV) → Green Revolution | Formulated 1965 | Third; Green Revolution manifested in the Annual Plans 1966-69
  [10:3:0.2] Drought Prone Area Programme (DPAP) | 1973 | Fourth
  [10:3:0.3] Minimum Needs Programme; directed anti-poverty programmes | 1974 (first year) | Fifth
  [10:3:0.4] Command Area Development (CADA) | 1974-75 | Fifth
  [10:3:0.5] Twenty-Point Programme | 1 July 1975 | Fifth
  [10:3:0.6] Food for Work | 1977 | Fifth (Janata)
  [10:3:0.7] IRDP (1978/1980), NREP (1980), MADA (tribal areas) | 1980 | Sixth
  [10:3:0.8] NABARD | 12 July 1982 | Sixth
  [10:3:0.9] RLEGP | 1983 | Sixth
  [10:3:0.10] Jawahar Rozgar Yojana (merged NREP, RLEGP) | 1 April 1989 | Seventh
  [10:3:0.11] Women's Component Plan (major strategy); 7 Basic Minimum Services | 1997 | Ninth (concept entered in Seventh)
  [10:3:0.12] Sarva Shiksha Abhiyan | 2001 | Ninth
  [10:3:0.13] Monitorable targets | 2002 | Tenth
  [10:3:0.14] National Horticulture Mission (May 2005), NRHM (2005) | 2005 | Tenth (not Eleventh)
  [10:3:0.15] RKVY | 2007 | Eleventh
  [10:3:0.16] MGNREGS | 2006 | Tenth
[10:3:1] - **Target-group** programmes (SFDA, MFAL, DPAP) began in the Fourth Plan.
[10:3:2] - **Chronology:** DPAP (1973) → IRDP (1978-80) → JRY (1989) → MGNREGS (2006).
[10:3:3] - **NRHM:** a functional health system; drinking water and sanitation integral; launched 2005 in the **Tenth** Plan.
[10:3:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.15-20; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.25; NextGen Rocket Sheets Indian Economy p.2-3, #16 p.22; app notes 3:10 sub 4
[10:3:5] - **Decentralised planning:** formally introduced in the **Seventh Plan** (1984 Working Group on District Planning, Hanumantha Rao).
[10:3:6] - **KVIC:** set up April 1957 under the KVIC Act 1956 – **Second Plan**.
[10:3:7] - **Panchayati Raj:** suggested by the **Balwant Rai Mehta** Committee (1957) – **Second Plan**; began at Nagaur, 2 Oct 1959.
[10:3:8] - **Twenty-Point Programme** (1 July 1975) – launched by PM **Indira Gandhi**; restructured in 1982, 1986 and 2006.

  [10:4:0.0] First | 1951-56 | Started 1 April 1951 = start of planning
  [10:4:0.1] Second | 1956-61 | Same year as IPR 1956
  [10:4:0.2] Third | 1961-66 | Launched 1961
  [10:4:0.3] Annual Plans | 1966-67 to 1968-69 | Plan Holiday 1966-69
  [10:4:0.4] Fourth | 1969-74 | Launched 1969
  [10:4:0.5] Fifth | 1974-79 | Launched 1974; ended 1978

[10:4:1] - **Wrong pair asked:** II Plan : 1966-71 ✗ (it was 1956-61).

[10:4:3] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.6-23; NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 02 p.1-12; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.44; NextGen Rocket Sheets Indian Economy #22 p.32
## SUB 5 | Second Plan Outlay; Eleventh and Twelfth Plan Targets
[10:5:0] - **Second Plan:** planned public outlay **Rs 4,800 crore** (private Rs 3,100 crore); actual **Rs 4,600 crore** (Rs 3,650 crore investment + Rs 950 crore current).
[10:5:1] - **Why pruned:** food and capital shortages, **inflation**, closed-economy assumption, **1957-58 foreign-exchange crisis** (not falling inflation).
[10:5:2] TABLE head: Criticism of the Nehru-Mahalanobis strategy | Detail
  [10:5:2.0] Agriculture | Neglected → food shortages
  [10:5:2.1] Employment | Capital-intensive industry → few jobs
  [10:5:2.2] Prices | High inflation by the late 1950s
[10:5:3] # Eleventh vs Twelfth Plan
[10:5:4] TABLE head: Feature | Eleventh (2007-12) | Twelfth (2012-17)
  [10:5:4.0] GDP target | 9% (10% by end) | 9% → 8%
  [10:5:4.1] Agriculture | 4% | 4%
  [10:5:4.2] Health | IMR 28; MMR 1 per 1,000 live births; TFR 2.1 | IMR 25
  [10:5:4.3] Targets | 27 national monitorable (13 State-level) | 25 monitorable
  [10:5:4.4] Outlay | Rs 36,44,718 crore | Rs 76,69,807 crore
[10:5:5] - **Eleventh inclusive growth:** continued reform and a competitive private sector (**not** withdrawal of the private sector); agriculture, health, education, skills, infrastructure; **7 crore** new jobs; poverty ratio down **10 points**.
[10:5:6] - **Twelfth:** fewer CSS with a **flexi-fund**; social-sector PPPs; plan/non-plan distinction reviewed by the **Rangarajan Committee**; largest allocation to **social services (~34.7%)**.
[10:5:7] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.8-9; NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 02 p.6-12; app notes 3:10 sub 6, 7
[10:5:8] - **Eleventh Plan sector order (total outlay):** social services first, then **energy**, then transport (15.7%).
[10:5:9] - **Tenth Plan allocation:** highest to **energy (26.5%)**, then social services (22.8%) and transport (14.3%).
[10:5:10] - **Eleventh Plan jobs:** largest growth expected in **construction** (~8.3% a year), then transport and communication (7.6%).
[10:5:11] - **Twelfth Plan education target:** raise the **mean years of schooling to seven**.
[10:5:12] - **Eleventh Plan monitorable targets** also included **broadband connectivity to all villages by 2012** and a **5-percentage-point rise in forest and tree cover**.
[10:5:13] - **Eleventh Plan combined Centre + State + UT outlay:** **social sector Rs 11,02,327 crore (>30%)** – the largest.
[10:5:14] - **9.5%:** the draft Approach Paper's figure for the last year (2011-12) and **AP's** Eleventh Plan GSDP target; the national average target was **9%**.
[10:5:15] - **Eleventh Plan total outlay (Centre + States):** social services ~30%, **energy Rs 8,54,123 crore (23.4%)** – the largest single head; education's 19.3% is of the Centre's budget support only.
[10:5:16] - **Twelfth Plan financing (public sector):** largest source = **borrowings incl. net miscellaneous capital receipts** (Rs 36.99 lakh crore of Rs 80.50 lakh crore), then balance from current revenues (Rs 23.47 lakh crore), then PSE resources (Rs 20.03 lakh crore).
[10:5:17] - **Tenth Plan actual spending:** highest on the **social sector (32.2%)**; energy only 16.6% despite its 26.5% allocation.
[10:5:18] - **Eleventh Plan education theme:** **compulsory (universal) elementary education** – dropouts 52.2% → 20% by 2011-12.
## SUB 6 | Andhra Pradesh and the Five Year Plans
[10:6:0] TABLE head: Project / programme | Fact
  [10:6:0.0] Nagarjuna Sagar | Foundation by Nehru, 10 Dec 1955; built 1955-67
  [10:6:0.1] Srisailam | Construction began 1960; opened 1981
  [10:6:0.2] Telugu Ganga | 1980s; Krishna water reached Poondi (Chennai) in 2004
  [10:6:0.3] Jalayagnam | 2004 irrigation mission to complete major and medium projects
  [10:6:0.4] Prakasam Barrage | Built 1954-57 across the Krishna at Vijayawada (old anicut 1852-55); named after Tanguturi Prakasam
[10:6:1] - **AP in the Eleventh Plan:** social sector first, education highest; GSDP target **9.5%**, achieved **5.28%**; industry **7.26%** vs **10%**; agriculture and allied **21.27%** of GSDP.
[10:6:2] - **Gadgil formula (1969)** → **Gadgil-Mukherjee (1991)**: population (**1971** Census) **60%**, per capita income **25%**, fiscal performance **7.5%**, special problems **7.5%**.
[10:6:3] - **Special Category** = **90% grant : 10% loan**; **General Category** (AP) = **30 : 70**.
[10:6:4] - **After the plans:** NITI Aayog from 1 Jan 2015; plans ended **2017**; **Swarna Andhra Vision 2047** launched **13 Dec 2024** – regions Visakhapatnam, Amaravati, **Tirupati**; NABARD AP State Focus Paper 2026-27 credit plan **Rs 5.11 lakh crore**.
[10:6:5] ~~ Sources: NextGen AP Economy Static-1 Part 1-2; app notes 3:10 sub 10
[10:6:6] - **AP Fifth Plan (1974-79):** Minimum Needs Programme, 20-point (1975) and 6-point formula, **CADA (Aug 1974)**.
[10:6:7] - **AP Tenth Plan:** irrigation first (Jalayagnam), social services second, power third; **industries** lowest.
[10:6:8] - **Shift in AP's Fourth Plan:** irrigation led the II and III Plans; **electricity** got prominence in the IV.
[10:6:9] - **Tenth Plan grouping of States:** A high income, **B middle income (AP, TN, Kerala, Karnataka, Rajasthan)**, C low income, D special category.
[10:6:10] - **AP Second Plan (1956-61):** highest priority **irrigation**, then electricity.
[10:6:11] - **AP plans:** **irrigation and power (energy)** led the outlay in plan after plan.
[10:6:12] - **AP Fourth Plan (1969-74):** first priority **electricity (energy)**, second irrigation.
## SUB 7 | Plans Cut Short, Plan Holiday and the Rolling Plan
[10:7:0] TABLE head: Plan | Cause | Effect
  [10:7:0.0] Third | 1962 and 1965 wars + 1965-66 drought | Plan Holiday (1966-69) declared after the Third Plan
  [10:7:0.1] Fifth | Emergency, new elections, Janata Party elected; Janata government did not want to continue it | Terminated a year early (1978); Rolling Plan (1978-80)
  [10:7:0.2] Eighth | 1990-91 political instability + BoP crisis | Postponed two years; Annual Plans 1990-92
[10:7:1] - **Rolling Plan** = the Janata government's **Sixth Plan (1978-83)**, advocated by Gunnar Myrdal; emphasis on **employment**; ended 1980; Congress launched a new Sixth Plan. Not introduced in any of the Third, Fourth or Fifth Plans.
[10:7:2] - Rolling Plan structure: a plan for **one year**, a plan for **3, 4 or 5 years** renewed every year, and a **perspective plan** for 10, 15 or 20 years.
[10:7:3] - **Two wars in one plan:** the Third (1962 China, 1965 Pakistan).
[10:7:4] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.10-17; NextGen Indian Economy Part 1 - APPSC 04 p.37, p.52; app notes 3:10 sub 2
[10:7:5] - **Janata Sixth Plan (1978-83):** never official; gave new life to **Panchayati Raj** (Asoka Mehta Committee 1977-78).
## SUB 8 | Achievements and Failures of the Plans
[10:8:0] TABLE head: Achievements | Failures
  [10:8:0.0] Steel plants Bhilai, Durgapur, Rourkela; Chittaranjan, Perambur; IITs | Persistent poverty and inequality; Eighth Plan – "the poor became poorer"
  [10:8:0.1] Food self-sufficiency – Green Revolution, FCI (1965), National Seeds Corporation | Job growth lagged (Eighth: 2% vs 2.6%)
  [10:8:0.2] SBI from the Imperial Bank (First Plan period); NABARD (1982) | Regional imbalance both across and within States
  [10:8:0.3] Poverty 48.3% (1977-78) → 37.4% (1983-84) | Eighth Plan public-sector share 34.3% vs 45.2% target
[10:8:1] ~~ Sources: NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 01 p.8, p.11, p.18, p.23; NextGen Indian Economy - APPSC 02 p.5; app notes 3:10 sub 8-9
[10:8:2] - **Each Five Year Plan** reviewed the previous plan and set future goals – 'an assessment of the past and a call for the future'.
[10:8:3] - **Agriculture** (with irrigation) kept a roughly steady share (~20-24%) of plan outlay; energy's share rose, transport's fell.
[10:8:4] - **'Decade of jobless growth':** the **1990s (1991-2000)** – organised employment lagged far behind GDP growth.
[10:8:5] - **Second Plan steel plants:** Bhilai (USSR), **Durgapur (UK)**, Rourkela (W. Germany).

  Which of the following was a major criticism of the Nehru–Mahalanobis strategy?

  x: The strategy was criticised for neglecting agriculture, generating food shortages and inflation, and relying on capital-intensive heavy industry that created few jobs.
